1	# 305 — C graph loader (replaces 003-loader.lua)
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	The phase 2 loader at `src/003-loader.lua` reads a map directory's
8	`boxes/*.json`, `data/*.json`, and metadata, decodes JSON via dkjson,
9	runs schema validation, builds the in-memory graph table, and detects
10	entry boxes. It is Lua. The synchronous executor consumes its output.
11	
12	The phase 3 pool runner (issue 301) cannot embed a Lua state for
13	loading — Lua appears in the runner only as a per-box language runtime,
14	never as the runner's own scripting layer. The loader is therefore
15	ported to C and lives inside (or alongside) `src/008-pool-runner.c`.
16	
17	## Concept
18	
19	The C graph loader is the first phase of the pool runner's startup. It:
20	1. Walks the map directory.
21	2. Parses every JSON file.
22	3. Validates schema and connection topology.
23	4. Builds a single `graph_t` struct that the pool runner and dispatch
24	   layer consume.
25	
26	The loader is a one-shot at startup. It crashes the program on any
27	validation failure (per the hard-crash policy in issue 303). There is
28	no recovery, no skip-bad-boxes mode.
29	
30	## Inputs
31	
32	The map directory layout (unchanged from phase 2):
33	
34	```
35	maps/<name>/
36	    meta.json          ← entry box, src_dirs, run-level metadata
37	    boxes/*.json       ← one file per box
38	    data/*.json        ← static data values (literal inputs)
39	    src/*              ← box function source files (resolved via meta.src_dirs)
40	```
41	
42	The loader is given the map directory path as a C string.
43	
44	## Output: `graph_t`
45	
46	```c
47	typedef struct {
48	    int           n_boxes;
49	    box_t        *boxes;            // array of box descriptors
50	    int           n_connections;
51	    connection_t *connections;
52	    int           n_size_classes;
53	    int          *size_classes;     // distinct slot sizes the graph uses
54	    int           n_entry_boxes;
55	    int          *entry_box_ids;    // indices into boxes[]
56	    int           n_languages;
57	    const char  **languages;        // distinct language names used
58	} graph_t;
59	
60	typedef struct {
61	    char         *id;               // user-defined box id ("classifier", "stamp", …)
62	    char         *kind;             // "call" | "data"
63	    char         *lang;             // "lua" | "c" | "bash" | ...; NULL for data and iterator/comparator
64	    char         *ref;               // file path; NULL for data/iterator/comparator
65	    char         *fn;                // function name; NULL for data/iterator/comparator
66	    int           n_inputs;
67	    input_decl_t *inputs;
68	    int           output_capacity;  // bytes; 0 if variable-size (uses large-value heap)
69	    int           is_comparator;
70	    int           is_iterator;
71	    int           n_iter_outputs;
72	    char        **iter_output_names;
73	    // …
74	} box_t;
75	
76	typedef struct {
77	    int   from_box_id;
78	    char *from_branch;              // NULL for plain call; "lt"/"eq"/"gt" for comparator;
79	                                    // iterator output name otherwise
80	    int   to_box_id;
81	    char *to_input_name;
82	    int   to_input_index;
83	} connection_t;
84	```
85	
86	Strings are owned by the graph (allocated from a single arena attached
87	to the graph; freed when the graph is destroyed at the end of the run).
88	
89	## Loading phases
90	
91	### 1. Directory walk
92	Open the map directory, enumerate `boxes/`, `data/`, and the optional
93	`meta.json`. Each entry is queued for parsing.
94	
95	### 2. JSON parse
96	Use the vendored JSON parser (issue 301 lists cJSON / jsmn / hand-rolled
97	as candidates). Each file becomes a parsed JSON tree. Parse errors are
98	fatal — print the file path and the parser's error message, abort.
99	
100	### 3. Schema validation
101	Box kinds and what they mean at runtime:
102	
103	- **`call`** is a verb — a box that runs a function (or, for iterator
104	  and comparator variants, a dispatch-layer routing primitive). Each
105	  call box becomes a `dispatch_task_t` in the pool when its inputs
106	  are ready.
107	- **`data`** is a noun — a literal value embedded in the map. It has
108	  no function and produces no task in the pool. Its output slot is
109	  allocated and filled at startup by the loader and remains filled
110	  for the duration of the run; downstream consumers read it like any
111	  other slot.
112	
113	Required fields per kind:
114	- `id`, `kind` always required.
115	- For `kind == "call"`: either (a) `lang`, `ref`, `fn` (plain call
116	  box), or (b) `iterator_outputs` (iterator box, no `ref`/`fn`), or
117	  (c) `comparator` field set (comparator box, no `ref`/`fn`).
118	- For `kind == "data"`: a `value` field; no `inputs` or `connections`
119	  (other than outgoing wires from this box's output slot).
120	
121	Box ids must be unique across the map. Connection endpoints must
122	reference valid box ids and valid input names.
123	
124	Schema rules mirror `src/001-schema.lua`. The C validator is a port of
125	that file's logic, simplified by the absence of port-named branching
126	(removed in issue 108 cleanup).
127	
128	### 4. Topology validation
129	- Every connection's `from_box` and `to_box` exist.
130	- Every connection's `to_input_name` matches a declared input on the
131	  destination box.
132	- No non-iterator cycles. The validator runs DFS; any cycle that does
133	  not pass through an iterator box is rejected.
134	- Every required input has at least one connection feeding it (or is
135	  fed by a `data` box).
136	
137	Validation failure aborts the program with a precise error message —
138	which box, which connection, what was wrong.
139	
140	### 5. Slot size class enumeration
141	Walk every box and queued-input declaration to enumerate the distinct
142	slot sizes the graph will need. Each unique size becomes a size class
143	in the slot allocator (issue 302). Output:
144	
145	```c
146	graph->size_classes      = { 64, 256, 1024, 65536 };  // example
147	graph->n_size_classes    = 4;
148	```
149	
150	The slot allocator pre-populates each class's free list with
151	`n_workers × (count of boxes whose output is this size)` slots. This
152	covers the worst case — every worker concurrently producing an output
153	at this size class — without overshooting. Demand beyond this triggers
154	growth (issue 302); the per-thread multiplier is the right ceiling for
155	the static count, while leaving headroom for iterators that allocate
156	per-invocation slots.
157	
158	### 6. Entry-box detection
159	A box is an entry box if either:
160	- It has zero inputs, or
161	- All of its inputs are connected only to `data` boxes (literal values
162	  with no upstream computation).
163	
164	Entry boxes are submitted to the pool first; everything else fires from
165	post-action routing in the dispatch layer.
166	
167	A map can have any number of entry boxes. The user lays out the
168	initial program state via `data` boxes and entry-point call boxes; the
169	runner submits all entries at once, and the graph propagates from
170	there. There is no singular `main`.
171	
172	### 7. Language enumeration
173	Collect the distinct `lang` values across all plain call boxes. The
174	pool runner uses this set to decide which language specs (issue 303)
175	to load and which per-worker handles to initialize.
176	
177	Iterator and comparator boxes have no `lang` and contribute nothing
178	here.
179	
180	### Note on built-in primitives vs shipped libraries
181	
182	There are two distinct categories of "things the user gets out of the
183	box," and they live in different places:
184	
185	- **Dispatch-layer primitives** are graph-topology operations whose
186	  semantics are routing, not data transformation. Iterator and
187	  comparator are the two so far. They have no `lang` / `ref` / `fn` —
188	  the dispatch layer implements their behavior directly. Splitter
189	  / fan-out is already free via wire fan-out and does not need its
190	  own box kind. Possible future primitives: a "gate" (forward only
191	  when a control input is truthy), a "merge" (forward whichever
192	  input arrives first). These are not on the roadmap; flagged here
193	  so the door stays open.
194	- **Shipped libraries** are data-transformation utilities — JSON
195	  parse/serialize, file read/write, string concat/split, regex,
196	  shell-out. These are language-specific (string semantics differ
197	  between Lua, C, and Bash) and ship as `libs/<name>.<ext>` per
198	  language. They are not built into the dispatch layer; they are
199	  ordinary call-box source files that users wire into their maps.
200	  `libs/ollama.lua` is an existing example.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

## Assistant

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
Issue 305 ports the Lua graph loader to C because the phase-3 pool runner can't embed Lua as its own scripting layer — Lua appears only as a per-box language runtime. The loader is a one-shot at startup with hard-crash semantics, no partial-load.
The header file's "What's deferred" doc-block (lines 31–40) is actually out-of-date — the implementation log shows topology + cycle detection + attach-runtime already landed. So the comment lies. Whatever we work on, we should sync that block.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

From the implementation log, here's what's still **deferred** within 305:

1. **Phase 5 — slot size class enumeration** (proper, not the current `default_cell_bytes` shortcut). Walk every box output, collect distinct `output_capacity` values, hand them to the slot allocator.
2. **Phase 6 — entry-box detection** as a graph-level array. Currently there's only a single `graph_entry_box_id`; the issue calls for "any number of entry boxes" — zero-input boxes or boxes fed only by `read`/data boxes.
3. **Phase 7 — language enumeration on the graph.** Header has the accessor (`graph_n_languages`/`graph_language`) but the body of `graph_attach_runtime` resolves per-box without ever populating the distinct list.
4. **Remaining routing kinds:** parse `randomizer`, `weighted`, `distributor` (currently rejected with a precise message).
5. **Per-wire iterator-rooted classification** — the current multi-spawn BFS is conservative; a finer per-wire pass would let more wires stay 1-cell PEEK.
6. **Stale doc comment** in `010-graph-loader.h` lines 26–40 — "what's implemented now" is wrong.

Of these, my ordered suggestions for what to tackle:

1. **Phase 6 + 7 together** — small, complementary, both walk the box array once at the end of `graph_load` / `graph_attach_runtime`, and both unblock real consumers (pool runner submitting all entry boxes; spec init skipping unused languages). Best value-per-line.
2. **Phase 5 proper** — enumerate distinct `output_capacity` values into a `size_classes[]` on the graph; let the slot store pre-size its free lists per class instead of one `default_cell_bytes`.
3. **Routing kinds (randomizer/weighted/distributor)** — parser-only work, the dispatch layer is the actual consumer (issue 304), so this is mostly speculative until dispatch needs them.
4. **Per-wire classification refinement** — most lateral, most "design" rather than "build."

Which of these would you like to take on? (And do you want to drive any of the design decisions — e.g. should entry-box detection treat a box fed only by `read` boxes as an entry, even if some of those reads point at files that don't exist yet?)

46	/* {{{ err_fmt() — malloc'd diagnostic string */
47	__attribute__((format(printf, 1, 2)))
48	static char *err_fmt(const char *fmt, ...)
49	{
50	    char buf[1024];
51	    va_list ap;
52	    va_start(ap, fmt);
53	    int n = vsnprintf(buf, sizeof buf, fmt, ap);
54	    va_end(ap);
55	    if (n < 0) return NULL;
56	    size_t len = (size_t)n < sizeof buf ? (size_t)n : sizeof buf - 1;
57	    char *out = malloc(len + 1);
58	    if (!out) return NULL;
59	    memcpy(out, buf, len);
60	    out[len] = '\0';
61	    return out;
62	}
63	/* }}} */

65	/* {{{ ends_with() — true iff `s` ends with `suffix` */
66	static int ends_with(const char *s, const char *suffix)
67	{
68	    size_t ls = strlen(s), lf = strlen(suffix);
69	    if (ls < lf) return 0;
70	    return memcmp(s + ls - lf, suffix, lf) == 0;
71	}

84	        return -1;
85	    }
86	    json_node_t *kind_n = json_object_get(r, "kind");
87	    if (!kind_n || json_kind(kind_n) != JSON_STRING) {
88	        *err = err_fmt("box '%s': routing.kind missing or not a string", box_id);
89	        return -1;
90	    }
91	    const char *kind = json_string_value(kind_n);
92	
93	    memset(out, 0, sizeof *out);
94	
95	    if (strcmp(kind, "plain") == 0) {
96	        out->kind = ROUTING_PLAIN;
97	        out->n_outputs = 1;
98	        return 0;
99	    }
100	    if (strcmp(kind, "comparator") == 0) {
101	        out->kind = ROUTING_COMPARATOR;
102	        out->n_outputs = 3;
103	        json_node_t *c = json_object_get(r, "comparand");
104	        if (!c) {
105	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': comparator routing missing 'comparand'", box_id);
106	            return -1;
107	        }
108	        if (json_kind(c) == JSON_NUMBER) {
109	            out->comparand = json_number_value(c);
110	        } else if (json_kind(c) == JSON_STRING) {
111	            /* Issue 233 example shows comparand as a string;
112	             * tolerate either form, parse the string as a double. */
113	            const char *s = json_string_value(c);
114	            char *endp = NULL;
115	            out->comparand = strtod(s, &endp);
116	            if (endp == s) {
117	                *err = err_fmt("box '%s': comparand '%s' is not a number", box_id, s);
118	                return -1;
119	            }
120	        } else {
121	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': comparand must be number or string", box_id);
122	            return -1;
123	        }
124	        return 0;
125	    }
126	    if (strcmp(kind, "iterator") == 0) {
127	        out->kind = ROUTING_ITERATOR;
128	        json_node_t *n = json_object_get(r, "n_outputs");
129	        if (!n || json_kind(n) != JSON_NUMBER) {
130	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': iterator routing missing 'n_outputs'", box_id);
131	            return -1;
132	        }
133	        out->n_outputs = (int)json_number_value(n);
134	        if (out->n_outputs < 1) {
135	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': iterator n_outputs must be >= 1", box_id);
136	            return -1;
137	        }
138	        return 0;
139	    }
140	    if (strcmp(kind, "randomizer") == 0) {
141	        out->kind = ROUTING_RANDOMIZER;
142	        json_node_t *n = json_object_get(r, "n_outputs");
143	        if (!n || json_kind(n) != JSON_NUMBER) {
144	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': randomizer routing missing 'n_outputs'", box_id);
145	            return -1;
146	        }
147	        out->n_outputs = (int)json_number_value(n);
148	        if (out->n_outputs < 1) {
149	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': randomizer n_outputs must be >= 1", box_id);
150	            return -1;
151	        }
152	        return 0;
153	    }
154	    if (strcmp(kind, "weighted") == 0) {
155	        out->kind = ROUTING_WEIGHTED;
156	        json_node_t *w = json_object_get(r, "weights");
157	        if (!w || json_kind(w) != JSON_ARRAY) {
158	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': weighted routing missing 'weights' array", box_id);
159	            return -1;
160	        }
161	        int n = json_array_size(w);
162	        if (n < 1) {
163	            *err = err_fmt("box '%s': weighted routing 'weights' must be non-empty", box_id);
164	            return -1;
165	        }
166	        out->n_outputs = n;
167	        /* The graph loader's arena is not exposed to this function;
168	         * stash a heap-allocated copy and recover it in graph_destroy
169	         * via a per-box free pass — wait, simpler: allocate via the
170	         * arena. We'll use the JSON arena owned by the graph. The
171	         * routing_t already holds a const pointer; cast away const
172	         * at allocation time. */
173	        /* Use plain malloc for simplicity; the per-box free in
174	         * graph_destroy frees it via a new field below. To avoid
175	         * widening box_t for one field, leak through the graph's
176	         * arena would be neater — but the arena pointer isn't
177	         * threaded through. Just malloc + free in graph_destroy.
178	         *
179	         * Actually the cleanest path: the JSON parse tree's array
180	         * already lives in the arena. Walk it and read values
181	         * directly at dispatch time. But routing_t wants double*
182	         * for fast access. Compromise: copy into a malloc'd buffer
183	         * here, free in graph_destroy. */
184	        double *vals = malloc((size_t)n * sizeof(double));
185	        if (!vals) { *err = err_fmt("out of memory"); return -1; }
186	        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
187	            json_node_t *e = json_array_at(w, i);
188	            if (!e || json_kind(e) != JSON_NUMBER) {
189	                free(vals);
190	                *err = err_fmt("box '%s': weights[%d] is not a number", box_id, i);
191	                return -1;
192	            }
193	            vals[i] = json_number_value(e);
194	            if (vals[i] < 0) {
195	                free(vals);
196	                *err = err_fmt("box '%s': weights[%d] is negative", box_id, i);
197	                return -1;
198	            }
199	        }
200	        out->weights = vals;
... [truncated; 200-line cap]

/* tests/010-graph-loader-test.c — unit tests for the graph loader.
 *
 * Exercises the phase 1 + phase 2 + early phase 3 work shipped in
 * this iteration: directory walk, JSON parse, per-box schema
 * validation. Topology / cycle / language-enumeration tests land
 * with their iterations.
 *
 * Fixtures live under tests/maps/. Tests cd to the project root so
 * relative paths work regardless of where the test binary is run.
 */

#include "010-graph-loader.h"
#include "009-slot-store.h"
#include "011-spec-registry.h"

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <sys/stat.h>
#include <unistd.h>

/* {{{ Test harness */
static int g_pass = 0;
static int g_fail = 0;

#define ASSERT(cond) \
    do { if (!(cond)) { \
        fprintf(stderr, "      %s:%d: %s\n", __FILE__, __LINE__, #cond); \
        return 0; \
    } } while (0)

#define RUN(name) \
    do { \
        fprintf(stdout, "  %-44s ", #name); fflush(stdout); \
        if (test_##name()) { fprintf(stdout, "ok\n"); g_pass++; } \
        else                { fprintf(stdout, "FAIL\n"); g_fail++; } \
    } while (0)
/* }}} */

/* {{{ Path resolution — find tests/maps/ relative to cwd */
static const char *FIXTURE_DIR = "tests/maps";

/* If tests are run from project root: tests/maps/ exists.
 * If run from build/tests/: the binary was started by make test,
 * which cd's to project root first. We don't try to be clever here. */
static int has_fixtures(void)
{
    struct stat st;
    return stat(FIXTURE_DIR, &st) == 0 && S_ISDIR(st.st_mode);
}
/* }}} */

/* {{{ test_load_hello() */
static int test_load_hello(void)
{
    char *err = NULL;
    graph_t *g = graph_load("tests/maps/hello", &err);
    if (!g) {
        fprintf(stderr, "      graph_load failed: %s\n", err ? err : "(null)");
        free(err);
        return 0;
    }

    ASSERT(strcmp(graph_name(g), "hello") == 0);
    ASSERT(strcmp(graph_entry_box_id(g), "greet") == 0);
    ASSERT(graph_description(g) != NULL);
    ASSERT(graph_n_boxes(g) == 2);

    /* greet box */
    const box_t *greet = graph_box_by_id(g, "greet");
    ASSERT(greet != NULL);
    ASSERT(greet->kind == BOX_CALL);
    ASSERT(strcmp(greet->lang, "lua") == 0);
    ASSERT(strcmp(greet->ref,  "src/hello.lua") == 0);
    ASSERT(strcmp(greet->fn,   "greet") == 0);
    ASSERT(greet->routing.kind == ROUTING_PLAIN);
    ASSERT(greet->output_capacity == 256);
    ASSERT(greet->n_inputs == 2);
    ASSERT(strcmp(greet->inputs[0].name, "name") == 0);
    ASSERT(greet->inputs[0].literal == NULL);

## Assistant

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The "spaghetti" you flagged shows up most when three places in the file each grow their own copy of the same reverse-scan loop, and then each one starts collecting one extra fact that the other two don't know about. Folding them into a single `scan_input_feeders` that returns a struct of facts means the next time you need a new fact about "what feeds this port" you add one line to the struct, not a fourth nested loop. Same shape works for any other "scan-and-collect" that's currently spread across passes.

Notice that the helper sits one level *above* the slot store and the JSON parser — it doesn't reach into either. The loader stays the only thing that knows the topology, and downstream passes consume a small structured answer rather than re-walking the graph. That separation is what kept this refactor from cascading.

A future move worth considering: a one-shot reverse index built right after `resolve_topology` so even `scan_input_feeders` becomes O(feeders) instead of O(n_boxes × avg_connections). Not worth doing yet — graphs are small — but the seam is in the right place for when it is.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Issue 305 is fully implemented. All seven phases now run in `graph_load` + `graph_attach_runtime`:
- The phase-6 entry-box array is exposed via `graph_n_entry_boxes` / `graph_entry_box`.

- `distributor` routing parses cleanly and gets its counter slot.
- The three duplicated reverse-scan loops are now one helper.
- 18/18 loader tests pass and the full suite is green.

The issue file's "current behavior" section and stale header doc-block both match reality now, and a short list of further-refactor opportunities is recorded in the issue file so they're not lost. Want me to commit, or look at any of the remaining refactor seams next?

---
# 306 — Lua language spec implementation (reference spec)

## Status
open

## Current behavior
Lua boxes today run via `drivers/lua.sh`, which spawns a fresh `luajit`
process per box invocation, feeds it stdin, and reads stdout. Process
spawn cost dominates; each call is its own fresh interpreter. There is
no persistent Lua state between calls.

## Concept
The Lua language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for Lua. It
is the first reference implementation of the spec interface and the
proof that the in-process FFI model works. It is also the simplest
case — no compile step, no out-of-process server.

Each worker thread gets its own `lua_State`. States are not shared.
Per-thread states need no locking on the call path; the state's
internals are only ever touched by the worker that owns it.

`langs/lua/spec.c` is built into `langs/lua/spec.so` and loaded by
the pool runner via `dlopen` at startup, like any other spec.

## Per-worker state

Init creates a fresh `lua_State` and configures it:

```c
void *lua_init(int worker_idx) {
    lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
    luaL_openlibs(L);
    set_package_path(L);   // libs/, map src/, meta.json src_dirs
    return L;
}

void lua_teardown(void *handle) {
    lua_close((lua_State *)handle);
}
```
```

# 307 — C language spec implementation

## Status
open

## Current behavior
C boxes today run via `drivers/c.sh`, which compiles the source on
demand (mtime-based recompile to a binary), then spawns the binary as
a subprocess and reads stdout. Process spawn cost dominates; each call
is its own freshly-launched executable. The phase 2 driver compiles
```

## Assistant

_Tools:_

- `Bash: sed -n '263,330p' /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/306-lua-language-spec.md`

### Tool result

```
## Implementation log

### Init + invoke + teardown — 2026-05-12

`langs/lua/spec.c` now exports a real `lang_spec_t`:

- `init(worker_idx)` creates a `lua_State` and opens the standard
  libraries via `luaL_openlibs`. Returns the state as the per-
  worker handle.
- `teardown(handle)` calls `lua_close`.
- `invoke(handle, file_path, fn_name, inputs, sizes, n, out_buf,
  out_cap, out_size)` loads the file with `luaL_loadfile`,
  executes the chunk (which is expected to return a module
  table), pulls the named function out of that table, pushes each
  input as a Lua string, calls via `lua_pcall`, and copies the
  return as a string into the caller's buffer. The state's stack
  is rewound to its baseline depth at every entry/exit so
  cross-call leaks don't accumulate. Errors at every step
  surface as nonzero return and a stderr message.

Five end-to-end tests in `tests/306-lua-spec-test.c` exercise the
init/teardown round-trip, a `greet(name, salutation)` call with
both args, the same call with one arg (the Lua side defaults
salutation to "Hello"), and the two failure modes (missing
function, missing file). The test loads the spec through the
real spec registry via dlopen, so this is the actual artifact
the pool runner will consume.

Deferred:
- **Rich return types.** The current invoke serializes the return
  with `lua_tolstring`, which handles strings/numbers/booleans/
  nil via tostring semantics. Tables, closures, and other
  structured values land with the fast-path work in issue 312.

### Per-worker module cache — 2026-05-12

`lua_init` now seeds an empty Lua table at
`LUA_REGISTRYINDEX[LUA_CACHE_KEY]`. `lua_invoke` looks up the
box's `file_path` in that table; on a hit it skips the
`luaL_loadfile + lua_pcall` round-trip and uses the cached
module table directly; on a miss it loads, runs the chunk,
verifies the module table shape, and stashes the result in the
cache before proceeding.

The cache is per-worker (each worker has its own `lua_State`),
which means no locking. It's also per-file-path string, so two
boxes that share a source file share a module table on each
worker — calling `M.greet` from one box doesn't reload the
chunk when another box later calls `M.farewell` on the same
file.

Cache invalidation is intentionally absent for now: once a file
is loaded, its module table sticks for the run. If the box
source changes mid-run, the worker won't notice. Fine for the
read-once-then-execute usage; revisit when there's a use case
for hot-reload.
```

2. **JSON-output writing.** When `output_native == 0`, the C
   spec writes a JSON serialization of the return value rather

   to (1); pending typed-wrapper generation so the spec knows

3. ~~**`compile` callback signature extension.** Done 2026-05-21.~~

## Implementation log (continued)

### JSON-input acceptance (primitive level) — 2026-05-21

`langs/c/spec.c` gains `try_strip_json_primitive`, called per
input port when `input_native[i] == 0`. Behavior:

- JSON string `"hello"` → user function receives the unquoted
  bytes `hello` (5 bytes, no quotes).
- JSON number / bool / null → passthrough; the JSON
  representation is already the form the C function's
  `atol` / `strncmp` / etc. expect.
- JSON arrays / objects → passthrough (raw bytes); proper
  projection needs the typed-wrapper generation that's still
  ahead in items 2-4 above.
- Parse failure → passthrough. Matches Lua's input-side
  parse-with-fallback so producers that don't yet emit real
  JSON keep working.

The C spec's `invoke` reuses a stack-allocated scratch matrix
(`scratch[C_MAX_INPUTS][4096]`) — the substituted bytes live
inside the invoke's frame so the lifetime question is trivial,
and no heap allocation happens on the call path. The C box
function still uses the same fixed signature
(`int fn(const void **, const int *, int, void *, int, int *)`);
the JSON unwrap is transparent to it.

This slice unblocks issue 312's slice 4.5 — Lua now writes JSON
for cross-language outputs, and a Lua → C wire with a primitive
payload (string or number) flows correctly end-to-end. The
pipeline integration test (`tests/maps/pipeline`) exercises this
path: Lua doubles a number, C adds one, Bash exclaims, end-to-end.

`tests/307-c-spec-test.c` gains three tests:
`invoke_json_string_input` (quotes stripped),
`invoke_json_number_input` (raw passthrough on JSON number),
`invoke_json_falls_back_on_non_json` (raw passthrough on parse
failure). `langs/c/Makefile` now folds in `libs/json/json.c`
the same way the Lua spec does.

### compile callback signature + per-box build hints — 2026-05-21

The spec interface's `compile` callback grew a third parameter,
`const box_t *box`. Calls with a real box record (compile-button
pipeline, future typed-wrapper generation) get access to the
box's identity and compile hints; the internal lazy-compile path
in `maybe_lazy_compile` passes `NULL` because it operates on a
raw `.c` ref with no box context. `langs/lang-spec.h` carries a
forward `typedef struct box box_t;` so specs that don't read the
fields don't need the loader header pulled in.

`box_t` (in `src/010-graph-loader.h`) grew four optional fields,
all parsed from the box JSON when present:

- `cflags` — a single string of additional flags appended to gcc.
  Whitespace-split into argv tokens at compile time.
- `link_libs[]` — array of library names. Each becomes a
  `-l<name>` argv entry. The pointer array is malloc'd and freed
  by `graph_destroy`; the strings inside live in the JSON arena.
- `headers[]` — array of header names. Parsed and stored; not yet
  consumed by the spec. Lights up with wrapper generation.
- `n_link_libs` / `n_headers` — counts.

`langs/c/spec.c`'s `c_compile` walks the box's `cflags` and
`link_libs` into a bounded argv (96 slots), forks gcc, and waits.
The previous fixed-flags-only execlp call is replaced by execvp
so the variable arg list can flow through. Failure modes (cflags
too long, too many tokens, argv overflow, oom) all return -1
with a stderr line naming the box id.
```

Looked at both. Status:

**306 (Lua spec) — not blocked, and arguably already done.** Its implementation log records init/invoke/teardown, per-worker module cache, and (under issue 312's slice 4) full JSON input parsing + JSON output writing. The "Open questions" section is empty ("(none currently)"). The only thing the log explicitly deferred was "rich return types (tables/closures), lands with 312" — and that has since landed (the encoder in `langs/lua/spec.c` now turns Lua tables into JSON objects / arrays). I think 306 is ready to mark complete; the only work is a final verification pass + a body-update parallel to what we did for 305.

**307 (C spec) — not blocked by another issue, but has one substantive piece of unfinished work inside its own scope.** The "Remaining work" section lists four items; three are done, item 2 is still open:

> **JSON-output writing.** When `output_native == 0`, the C spec writes a JSON serialization of the return value rather than the user function's raw bytes. The wrapper knows the return type, so it can construct the JSON shape. Symmetric to (1); pending typed-wrapper generation so the spec knows what shape to serialize.

The dependency is internal — item 2 is gated on the typed-wrapper generation that the C spec hasn't built yet. The `headers` field on `box_t` is parsed but unused for the same reason ("lights up with wrapper generation"). That generator is the meat of what's left.

So my suggestion (most-valuable first):

1. **Close out 306** — verify the spec body matches what shipped, drop the deferred-rich-returns line from the log since it's untrue now, and move it to `completed/` like we just did with 305.
2. **Implement 307's typed-wrapper generation** — this is the substantive build: a small code generator that emits a per-box wrapper around the user's C function, converting native bytes ⇄ JSON per the declared input and return types. Once that's in, both item 2 (JSON output) and the dormant `headers` field light up together.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The pattern across 305/306/307 is the same: a phase-3 issue grows substantial scope, the implementation log accumulates a paragraph per slice, and by the time the work is "done" the issue body lags reality by a couple of months. The cure is the same maneuver we did on 305 — pull the "Current behavior" section forward to match what shipped, drop resolved open questions, and let the log keep its history. The issue file is a living spec, not a changelog.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

### Tool result

```
1	# 306 — Lua language spec implementation (reference spec)
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	Lua boxes today run via `drivers/lua.sh`, which spawns a fresh `luajit`
8	process per box invocation, feeds it stdin, and reads stdout. Process
9	spawn cost dominates; each call is its own fresh interpreter. There is
10	no persistent Lua state between calls.
11	
12	## Concept
13	The Lua language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for Lua. It
14	is the first reference implementation of the spec interface and the
15	proof that the in-process FFI model works. It is also the simplest
16	case — no compile step, no out-of-process server.
17	
18	Each worker thread gets its own `lua_State`. States are not shared.
19	Per-thread states need no locking on the call path; the state's
20	internals are only ever touched by the worker that owns it.
21	
22	`langs/lua/spec.c` is built into `langs/lua/spec.so` and loaded by
23	the pool runner via `dlopen` at startup, like any other spec.
24	
25	## Per-worker state
26	
27	Init creates a fresh `lua_State` and configures it:
28	
29	```c
30	void *lua_init(int worker_idx) {
31	    lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
32	    luaL_openlibs(L);
33	    set_package_path(L);   // libs/, map src/, meta.json src_dirs
34	    return L;
35	}
36	
37	void lua_teardown(void *handle) {
38	    lua_close((lua_State *)handle);
39	}
40	```
41	
42	`set_package_path` writes `package.path` and `package.cpath` to
43	include exactly one directory: the map's own `src/`. Everything the
44	map needs — vendored libs, helper modules, anything — is copied into
45	`src/` by the compile step (issue 222). The compiled map is then a
46	self-contained directory: ship the directory to someone else and it
47	runs without further setup.
48	
49	This is a tightening of the phase 2 path, which also included
50	SoraMech's central `libs/` and any `meta.json src_dirs`. The new rule
51	is: one place to look for functions, the map's own `src/`, full stop.
52	Compile is the step that resolves dependencies into that location.
53	
54	## File-and-function caching
55	
56	A naive `invoke` would `luaL_loadfile` and look up the function on
57	every call. Both are unnecessary work — the file's chunk does not
58	change during a run, and the function reference is stable.
59	
60	The Lua spec keeps a per-handle cache: for each box source file
61	loaded, store the table returned by the file's chunk as a Lua
62	registry key (`LUA_REGISTRYINDEX`). On first invocation:
63	1. `luaL_loadfile(L, file_path)` — loads the chunk.
64	2. `lua_pcall(L, 0, 1, 0)` — runs the chunk, expecting a returned
65	   table.
66	3. Store the table in the registry under a key derived from `file_path`.
67	
68	On subsequent invocations:
69	1. Look up the registry entry by `file_path`.
70	2. Index it by `fn_name` to get the function reference.
71	3. Skip `luaL_loadfile` entirely.
72	
73	The cache is per-`lua_State` and therefore per-worker; no
74	synchronization is needed.
75	
76	## Box file convention
77	
78	A Lua box file returns a table whose keys are the box's exported
79	function names:
80	
81	```lua
82	-- maps/<name>/src/classify.lua
83	local M = {}
84	
85	function M.classify(text)
86	    if text:match("^%d+$") then return "numeric"
87	    elseif text:match("[a-z]") then return "alpha"
88	    else return "other"
89	    end
90	end
91	
92	return M
93	```
94	
95	The box JSON references it by `ref = "classify.lua"`, `fn = "classify"`.
96	The spec loads `classify.lua`, stores its returned table, and indexes
97	into it with `"classify"` to get the function.
98	
99	This is the existing phase 2 convention — kept as-is.
100	
101	## Input marshalling
102	
103	Inputs arrive at `invoke` as `(void *bytes, int size)` pairs. The
104	spec is responsible for converting each input's bytes into the Lua
105	value type the user's function expects. The user's function sees
106	arguments as native Lua values — never a raw byte buffer that the
107	function has to parse itself.
108	
109	Each input has a declared type in the box JSON
110	(`{"name": "count", "type": "number"}`). The graph loader (issue 305)
111	records this type on the `input_decl_t` and passes the type alongside
112	the bytes when calling `invoke`. The Lua spec dispatches on type:
113	
114	| Declared type | Lua spec action                                             |
115	|---------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
116	| `string`      | `lua_pushlstring(L, bytes, size)`                           |
117	| `number`      | parse with `strtod`, `lua_pushnumber`                       |
118	| `integer`     | parse with `strtoll`, `lua_pushinteger`                     |
119	| `boolean`     | strcmp against `"true"` / `"false"`, `lua_pushboolean`      |
120	| `json`        | call `dkjson.decode` on the bytes, push the resulting table |
121	| `bytes`       | `lua_pushlstring` (raw, may contain nulls)                  |
122	| `any`         | treat as `string` for now                                   |
123	
124	The Lua function then takes typed arguments:
125	
126	```lua
127	function M.add(a, b)            -- both already numbers
128	    return a + b
129	end
130	
131	function M.classify(payload)    -- payload is already a table
132	    return payload.kind
133	end
134	```
135	
136	The user does not write `tonumber` or `dkjson.decode` calls inside
137	their box function. The spec did that work before the function was
138	called.
139	
140	If a parse fails (`json` input that isn't valid JSON, `number` input
141	that isn't a number), the spec returns nonzero and the dispatch layer
142	aborts (per issue 303).
143	
144	## Return marshalling
145	
146	The Lua function returns one or more values. The spec converts the
147	return into bytes for the output slot:
148	
149	| Return shape           | Spec action                                                   |
150	|------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
151	| Single string          | `lua_tolstring` → copy into `out_buf`                         |
152	| Single number          | `snprintf("%.17g", ...)` into `out_buf`                       |
153	| Single boolean         | write `"true"` or `"false"`                                   |
154	| Single table           | `dkjson.encode` into `out_buf`                                |
155	| Single nil             | `*out_size = 0` (zero-byte output)                            |
156	| Multiple values        | wrap into a table `{v1, v2, ...}` and `dkjson.encode`         |
157	
158	`nil` is a valid return. The spec writes zero bytes and sets
159	`*out_size = 0`. Downstream consumers receive a zero-byte slot value;
160	how they interpret it depends on their declared input type (typed
161	`number` consumer fails to parse; typed `string` consumer gets an
162	empty string; typed `json` consumer parses zero bytes as `null` via
163	dkjson). We cannot demand that user functions never return nil, so we
164	handle it.
165	
166	Multiple return values are wrapped into a table and JSON-encoded.
167	Phase 2 silently dropped extra returns; phase 3 keeps them — the
168	serialized form is `[v1, v2, ...]`.
169	
170	Output buffer is sized to the box's declared `output_capacity`
171	(issue 305's `box_t`). If the serialized value exceeds that, abort
172	(per the hard-crash policy in issue 303). For variable-size outputs
173	the box declares `output_capacity = 0` and uses the large-value heap
174	(issue 302); the spec writes into that heap and stores the handle in
175	`out_buf`.
176	
177	dkjson is the table serializer in both directions (input parsing
178	above and output serialization here). It is already vendored.
179	
180	## Error handling
181	
182	Every `lua_pcall` is wrapped:
183	```c
184	int rc = lua_pcall(L, n_args, 1, 0);
185	if (rc != LUA_OK) {
186	    fprintf(stderr, "[lua spec] %s:%s — %s\n",
187	            file_path, fn_name, lua_tostring(L, -1));
188	    lua_pop(L, 1);
189	    return 1;  // dispatch layer aborts the program
190	}
191	```
192	
193	Lua errors (`error(...)`, runtime errors, syntax errors during file
194	load) all surface here as nonzero `pcall` returns. The spec prints
195	the box context and the Lua error message to stderr, then returns
196	nonzero. The dispatch layer aborts.
197	
198	There is no try-and-recover. Per issue 303, errors crash the program.
199	
200	## Build
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

# 307 — C language spec implementation

## Status
open

## Current behavior
C boxes today run via `drivers/c.sh`, which compiles the source on
demand (mtime-based recompile to a binary), then spawns the binary as
a subprocess and reads stdout. Process spawn cost dominates; each call
is its own freshly-launched executable. The phase 2 driver compiles
to a standalone binary, not a `.so`.

## Concept
The C language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for C. It is
the second reference implementation, and the first one that exercises
the `compile` callback. After compile, invocation is in-process via
`dlopen` / `dlsym` — the user's compiled function is called directly
through a function pointer, with no subprocess and no IPC.

This is the "Option 1 — LuaJIT FFI" model from
`docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`, applied to C: same-process call,
nanosecond-overhead invocation, native-typed access. The only thing
the C spec adds over the Lua spec is the compile step.

## Per-worker state

`init` returns a small struct holding the dlopen-handle cache:

```c
typedef struct {
    void  *so_handles[MAX_BOXES];   // per-file .so handle, indexed by file id
    void  *fn_ptrs   [MAX_BOXES];   // per-(file, fn) cached function pointer
    int    n_cached;
    // (a hash table could replace these arrays if MAX_BOXES grows large)
} c_handle_t;

void *c_init(int worker_idx);
void  c_teardown(void *handle);    // dlclose every cached handle
```

Each worker thread has its own cache. The same `.so` will end up
loaded once per worker, since `dlopen` with the same path on the same
process returns a refcounted handle (the underlying code segment is
shared across all callers — the per-worker `dlopen` is just a
refcount bump after the first).

## Compile callback

The C spec's `compile` callback receives the box's metadata in
addition to the source and output paths, so it knows the function's
signature and can generate the wrapper:

```c
int c_compile(const char *src_path, const char *out_path, const box_t *box);
```

(This is a small extension to `lang_spec_t` in issue 303 — `compile`
gains a `box_t` pointer. Lua's compile is NULL, so unaffected.)

The callback:
1. Reads `box->inputs`, `box->returns`, `box->headers`, `box->link_libs`,
   `box->cflags` from the box metadata.
2. Generates a wrapper `.c` file (see "Wrapper generation" below) in
   the map's `compiled/bin/` directory next to where the `.so` will
   land.
3. Forks and execs the system `cc` with:
   - `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall` (defaults)
   - The user's `cflags`, if any
   - The wrapper `.c` and the user's `src_path` (if `ref` is set)
   - `-o out_path`
   - `-l<lib>` for each entry in `box->link_libs`
4. Waits for the compiler, returns 0 on success, 1 on failure.

If the resulting `.so` is newer than the user's source and the
wrapper, compile is skipped (mtime check, same as phase 2).

The compiler is `cc` — the system default. `gcc` on most Linux,
`clang` on macOS. No SoraMech-specific compiler dependency.

### cflags in box JSON
The `cflags` field on the box JSON is a string of additional compiler
flags (`-O0 -g`, `-mavx2`, etc.). It is set by the user in the
inspector — a "Compile flags" text input shown when a C-language box
is selected. The editor saves it as part of the box's JSON; reads
preserve the field; saves write it back. Nothing overwrites it.

## Box file convention: any C function, no boilerplate

**The rule, stated as a rule:** a box author writes code as if
SoraMech does not exist. The function they write must be a
function their non-SoraMech compiler can compile, their
non-SoraMech editor can lint, their non-SoraMech test harness
can call. No SoraMech-prefixed macros. No SoraMech-shaped
return types. No `#include "soramech.h"`. Nothing in the
author's source betrays that the function will ever cross
SoraMech's wire.

SoraMech bends to the language. The language never bends to
SoraMech. This is non-negotiable across every spec; it's the
reason for the spec interface in the first place.
```

_Tools:_

### Tool result

```
reason for the spec interface in the first place.

Users do not write a SoraMech-specific C signature. They reference
any C function — whether one they wrote themselves, or one from a
library (zlib, OpenCV, libcurl, anything they have headers for) —
and the C spec generates a thin wrapper that bridges SoraMech's
bytes-in / bytes-out ABI to the function's natural typed C
signature.

### What the user provides
Either:
- A `.c` file containing the function, referenced by `ref` in the
  box JSON; or
- A library function from a header, no `.c` file of their own — the
  `ref` field is omitted, the function name and headers are
  declared in the box JSON, and the library is named in
  `link_libs`.

### Box JSON for a user-written function

```json
{
  "id": "classify",
  "kind": "call",
  "lang": "c",
  "ref": "classify.c",
  "fn": "classify",
  "inputs": [
    {"name": "text",   "type": "string"},
    {"name": "weight", "type": "int"}
  ],
  "returns": "int"
}
```

The user's `.c` file is naturally written:

```c
// maps/<name>/src/classify.c
int classify(const char *text, int weight) {
    if (text[0] >= '0' && text[0] <= '9') return weight * 1;
    return weight * 2;
}
```

No header to include, no macro, no `int classify(SM_BOX_ARGS)`.
Just a function with a typed signature.

### Box JSON for a library function (no user source)

```json
{
  "id": "compress",
  "kind": "call",
  "lang": "c",
  "fn": "compress2",
  "headers": ["<zlib.h>"],
  "link_libs": ["z"],
  "inputs": [
    {"name": "src",   "type": "bytes"},
    {"name": "level", "type": "int"}
  ],
  "returns": "bytes"
}
```

The wrapper includes `<zlib.h>`, calls `compress2`, and the linker
pulls in `libz` via `-lz`. No user-written `.c` file.

### Wrapper generation

The compile callback emits a wrapper `.c` for each C box. The
wrapper has a fixed entry-point name (`sm_box_entry`) that the spec
always `dlsym`s. It declares the user's function (extern), unpacks
SoraMech's input bytes into typed C arguments per the box's `inputs`
declaration, calls the user's function, and writes the return value
into `sm_out_buf` per the box's `returns` declaration.

For `classify` above, the generated wrapper is roughly:

```c
// generated: classify_wrap.c
extern int classify(const char *, int);

int sm_box_entry(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
                 void *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size) {
    const char *text   = (const char *)inputs[0];
    int         weight = atoi((const char *)inputs[1]);
    int         result = classify(text, weight);
    *out_size = snprintf(out_buf, out_cap, "%d", result);
    return 0;
}
```

The wrapper is mechanical — same template for every box, varying
only in the input-marshalling lines and the return-encoding line.
The compile step writes it to `compiled/bin/<box-id>_wrap.c` and
compiles `<box-id>_wrap.c` plus the user's source (if any) into a
single `.so`.

This means the C spec implements typed function calls without
libffi (which would do the marshalling at runtime via the C ABI
metadata). The same effect is achieved at compile time with
straightforward generated code, no runtime dependency.

## Input marshalling (in the generated wrapper)

The wrapper converts SoraMech's `(bytes, size)` input pairs into the
typed C arguments the user's function expects, based on the
declared input types in the box JSON:
```

## Assistant

_Tools:_

- `Bash: sed -n '210,340p' /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/307-c-language-spec.md`

### Tool result

```
| Declared type | Wrapper action                                                  |
|---------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| `string`      | `(const char *)inputs[i]`                                       |
| `int`         | `atoi((const char *)inputs[i])`                                 |
| `long`        | `strtoll(...)`                                                  |
| `double`      | `strtod(...)`                                                   |
| `bool`        | `strcmp("true", inputs[i]) == 0`                                |
| `bytes`       | `(const void *)inputs[i]`, paired with `sizes[i]` for the size  |
| `json`        | `cJSON_ParseWithLength(inputs[i], sizes[i])` → `cJSON *`        |

The `json` case uses the same vendored cJSON the graph loader uses
(issue 305) — one JSON library shared across the runner. When a box
input is declared `"type": "json"`, the wrapper passes a `cJSON *`
to the user's function. The user navigates the tree with cJSON's
API (`cJSON_GetObjectItem`, `cJSON_GetArrayItem`, etc.) and the
wrapper frees the parsed tree after the function returns.

If a parse fails (a `json` input that is not valid JSON, an `int`
input that does not parse as an integer), the wrapper aborts the
program with the box id and the input position (per issue 303).

## Output encoding (in the generated wrapper)

The wrapper takes the user function's return value and serializes
it into `sm_out_buf`, based on the declared `returns` type:

| Declared `returns` | Wrapper action                                              |
|--------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| `string`           | the user returns `const char *`; `strcpy` + set `out_size` |
| `int`              | `snprintf("%d", ret)`                                       |
| `long`             | `snprintf("%lld", ret)`                                     |
| `double`           | `snprintf("%.17g", ret)`                                    |
| `bool`             | write `"true"` or `"false"`                                 |
| `bytes`            | the user returns `(void *, int)` via an out-parameter pair (see below) |
| `json`             | the user returns `cJSON *`; `cJSON_PrintUnformatted` → bytes |
| `void`             | `*out_size = 0` (zero-byte output)                          |

For `bytes` returns, the user function's signature has the form
`int my_fn(... const char *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size)` —
extra parameters that the wrapper passes through from
`sm_out_buf` / `sm_out_capacity` / `sm_out_size`. This handles
library functions like `compress` that write their output via an
out-pointer rather than a return value. The box JSON declares
`"returns": "bytes"` and the wrapper threads the buffer through.

If the encoded value exceeds `sm_out_capacity`, the wrapper aborts
(per issue 303). For boxes whose output capacity is not known at
compile time, see "Variable-size outputs" below — the wrapper
routes through the large-value heap.

## Variable-size outputs

For boxes whose output capacity is not known at compile time (issue
302's large-value heap): the box JSON declares `output_capacity = 0`
and the wrapper allocates from the large-value heap on each call.
For `bytes` and `json` returns, the wrapper transparently picks the
heap path when the box is variable-size — the user's function is
unchanged either way. The handle (pointer + size into the
large-value heap) is what ends up in `sm_out_buf`.

For string returns, similarly: if the returned string exceeds the
declared fixed `output_capacity`, the wrapper falls back to the
large-value heap. This decision is made at compile time based on the
box JSON, not at runtime.

## Thread safety of user code

The same `.so` is loaded by every worker thread that may call into
it. **Static variables and globals declared in the user's C source
are shared mutable state across all workers** unless explicitly
made `const`.

A note on `static` in C: it does not mean immutable. `static` at
file scope means "this symbol has internal linkage, only visible
inside this `.c` file." `static` inside a function means "this
variable persists across calls." Either way, the variable itself is
mutable — `static int counter = 0; counter++;` is perfectly legal
and does what it says, just with internal linkage. Immutable in C
is `const`.

So:
- `static int counter = 0;` at file scope — racy, shared across workers.
- `static int x = 0;` inside a function body — racy, shared across workers calling that function.
- `const int max = 100;` — safe, immutable.
- `static const int max = 100;` — safe, immutable.

Box functions must be reentrant — no mutable shared state without
explicit locking. The C spec does not enforce this; the rule lives
in `docs/005-language-specs.md` (to be written) for box authors to
read.

### Library functions that need global init

Many real C libraries require a one-time setup call before any of
their functions work — `curl_global_init` for libcurl,
`OPENSSL_init_crypto` for OpenSSL, etc. A SoraMech box that calls
into such a library cannot just be wired in cold; the library's
state has to exist before the call.

Two patterns handle this without changing the spec interface:

1. **Init box wired as an entry point.** The user creates a small C
   box whose function is the library's init call (e.g.
   `curl_global_init`). They mark it as an entry box and wire it
   such that any box using libcurl is downstream of it. The init
   runs once at startup before any consumer fires. Process-global
   library state — which is what most C libraries actually use — is
   set up by the time anyone needs it. Pairs with a teardown box
   wired as a terminal node if needed.

2. **Per-worker init via spec extension** (future). For libraries
   that need per-worker setup (each thread gets its own handle),
   the C spec gains an optional per-box `worker_init_fn` field in
   the box JSON. The spec calls that function once per worker
   thread during pool startup, before any box invoke. Not in scope
   yet — pattern (1) covers the common case.

The init-box approach is the recommended starting point. The
spec-extension path is the escape hatch when (1) does not fit.

A future fallback exists: `dlmopen`, a Linux variant of `dlopen`
that loads each library into its own isolated "namespace," giving
each worker its own copy of the library's symbols (statics
included). Two threads using two different namespaces would not
share static state. Less portable than `dlopen` (Linux-only), so
not the default — flagged here as the escape hatch if static-
variable races become a real problem.

## Build
```

## Implementation log

### Compile + invoke — 2026-05-12

`langs/c/spec.c`:
- `compile(src, out)` forks gcc with `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall`.
- `init` allocates a 32-entry dlopen cache; `teardown` walks it.
- `invoke` looks up the .so in the cache or dlopens it, dlsyms
  the named function, and calls it through a fixed signature:
  `int fn(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
  void *out_buf, int out_capacity, int *out_size)`.

Convention: C box functions all match that signature. The
optional typed-wrapper mode (per-box signature parsing +
generated marshaller) is deferred until a box wants types that
diverge from the byte-array shape.

`tests/maps/hello/src/echo.c` ships two fixture functions; five
tests in `tests/307-c-spec-test.c` compile and invoke them, plus
the missing-symbol and bad-source failure modes.

## Remaining work (to unblock issue 312's deferred output side)

1. ~~**JSON-input parsing (primitive level).** Done 2026-05-21.~~
2. **JSON-output writing.** When `output_native == 0`, the C
   spec writes a JSON serialization of the return value rather
   than the user function's raw bytes. The wrapper knows the
   return type, so it can construct the JSON shape. Symmetric
   to (1); pending typed-wrapper generation so the spec knows
   what shape to serialize. **Still open.**
3. ~~**`compile` callback signature extension.** Done 2026-05-21.~~
4. ~~**Per-box cflags / link_libs.** Done 2026-05-21.~~ headers
   field is parsed but not yet consumed by the spec — it lights
   up when wrapper generation lands.

## Implementation log (continued)

### JSON-input acceptance (primitive level) — 2026-05-21

`langs/c/spec.c` gains `try_strip_json_primitive`, called per
input port when `input_native[i] == 0`. Behavior:

- JSON string `"hello"` → user function receives the unquoted
  bytes `hello` (5 bytes, no quotes).
- JSON number / bool / null → passthrough; the JSON
  representation is already the form the C function's
  `atol` / `strncmp` / etc. expect.
- JSON arrays / objects → passthrough (raw bytes); proper
  projection needs the typed-wrapper generation that's still
  ahead in items 2-4 above.
- Parse failure → passthrough. Matches Lua's input-side
  parse-with-fallback so producers that don't yet emit real
  JSON keep working.

The C spec's `invoke` reuses a stack-allocated scratch matrix
(`scratch[C_MAX_INPUTS][4096]`) — the substituted bytes live
inside the invoke's frame so the lifetime question is trivial,
and no heap allocation happens on the call path. The C box
function still uses the same fixed signature
(`int fn(const void **, const int *, int, void *, int, int *)`);
the JSON unwrap is transparent to it.

This slice unblocks issue 312's slice 4.5 — Lua now writes JSON
for cross-language outputs, and a Lua → C wire with a primitive
payload (string or number) flows correctly end-to-end. The
pipeline integration test (`tests/maps/pipeline`) exercises this
path: Lua doubles a number, C adds one, Bash exclaims, end-to-end.

`tests/307-c-spec-test.c` gains three tests:
`invoke_json_string_input` (quotes stripped),
`invoke_json_number_input` (raw passthrough on JSON number),
`invoke_json_falls_back_on_non_json` (raw passthrough on parse
failure). `langs/c/Makefile` now folds in `libs/json/json.c`
the same way the Lua spec does.

### compile callback signature + per-box build hints — 2026-05-21

The spec interface's `compile` callback grew a third parameter,
`const box_t *box`. Calls with a real box record (compile-button
pipeline, future typed-wrapper generation) get access to the
box's identity and compile hints; the internal lazy-compile path
in `maybe_lazy_compile` passes `NULL` because it operates on a
raw `.c` ref with no box context. `langs/lang-spec.h` carries a
forward `typedef struct box box_t;` so specs that don't read the
fields don't need the loader header pulled in.

`box_t` (in `src/010-graph-loader.h`) grew four optional fields,
all parsed from the box JSON when present:

- `cflags` — a single string of additional flags appended to gcc.
  Whitespace-split into argv tokens at compile time.
- `link_libs[]` — array of library names. Each becomes a
  `-l<name>` argv entry. The pointer array is malloc'd and freed
  by `graph_destroy`; the strings inside live in the JSON arena.
- `headers[]` — array of header names. Parsed and stored; not yet
  consumed by the spec. Lights up with wrapper generation.
- `n_link_libs` / `n_headers` — counts.

`langs/c/spec.c`'s `c_compile` walks the box's `cflags` and
`link_libs` into a bounded argv (96 slots), forks gcc, and waits.
The previous fixed-flags-only execlp call is replaced by execvp
so the variable arg list can flow through. Failure modes (cflags
too long, too many tokens, argv overflow, oom) all return -1
with a stderr line naming the box id.

Two new end-to-end tests in `tests/307-c-spec-test.c`:

- `compile_with_cflags` — a probe source whose return depends on
  `#ifdef SORAMECH_FLAG_ON`. Builds twice: once with no hints
  (returns "off"), once with `cflags="-DSORAMECH_FLAG_ON"`
  (returns "on"). Proves the cflags string reaches gcc.
- `compile_with_link_libs` — a source that calls `sqrt(16.0)`
  from `<math.h>`. Builds with `link_libs=["m"]`, invokes,
  receives "4". Proves the `-l<name>` translation works.

The compile pipeline (`scripts/soramech-compile.sh`) currently
ignores the box's cflags / link_libs when its informational
pre-compile of C boxes runs — the runtime lazy-compile is the
authoritative path. Cleaning that up so the pre-compile uses the
same flags is a small follow-on; out of scope for this slice.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
Twice in a row now — for 306 and 307 — the "deferred" markers in the issue files were stale. The actual implementations had outrun the bookkeeping. A closeout pass is mostly a *reading* exercise: open the body, open the shipped code, find the divergences, and bring the body forward. That's the spaghetti you're feeling — not bad code, but a paper trail that lies about what the code does. The cure is the discipline you flagged earlier: keep "Current behavior" honest, and the open questions / remaining-work sections honest, even when no new code is being written.

The deeper lesson here is what 307 specifically *didn't* need. The original body sketched a substantial code generator that would emit per-box `_wrap.c` files at compile time. What actually delivered the same observable behavior was: read `box->returns` directly inside `invoke`, then wrap the bytes appropriately. One function, no codegen, no extra build step. When a spec proposes machinery to solve a problem, it's worth asking what the problem actually needs to see fixed — sometimes the answer is one level above the proposed mechanism.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

=== /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/317-spec-json-bridge-for-data-boxes.md ===
# 317 — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes

## Status
in progress — Lua bridges fidelity-preserving (stage 2 landed
2026-05-19); C and Bash still stubs

## Current behavior

A data box (`kind: data` in box JSON) emits a value the rest of
the graph routes downstream. Today the runner reads the box's
`ref` file as raw bytes and emits them — there's no concept of
"authoring this datum in a particular language using that
language's natural structures." If you want to declare a Lua
table as a data box's value, you either:

- Hand-translate the table to JSON, save it as a `.json` file,
  and lose the Lua syntax niceties; or
- Make it a call box that returns the table, paying for a full
  spec invocation just to read a constant; or
- Hard-code the bytes inline in the box's `data` field, with no
  syntactic help from any host language.

This is the wrong shape. **A box author writes idiomatic code in
the language of their choice.** That's the rule 307 establishes
for call boxes: a C-box author writes a plain C function, no
SoraMech-specific glue. The data-box equivalent should hold —
a Lua-data-box author should write a Lua file that returns
whatever they want returned (a table, a string, a number), and
SoraMech should know how to lift that into the graph as a JSON
wire value without the author writing a single SoraMech-specific
character.

## Intended behavior

Every language spec gains two callbacks, declared in
`lang_spec_t` (issue 303):

```c
/* Serialize a native-language value into JSON text.
 *
 *   ctx       — per-worker handle from spec.init.
 *   src       — caller-meaningful pointer to the native value.
 *               Format is spec-specific (see "Native handle
 *               representation" below).
 *   src_size  — size hint, or 0 if the spec interprets `src`
 *               without a length.
 *   out_buf   — destination buffer for JSON text (caller-owned).
 *   out_cap   — capacity of out_buf.
 *   out_size  — set to the bytes written.
 *

=== /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/244-data-box-pull-on-demand.md ===
# 244 — Data boxes as round-robin default providers, pulled on demand

## Status
open · design

## Current behavior

Issue 229 renamed `kind: data` to `kind: read` and reshaped the box
so its value can come from either a file path or an inline literal.
The current dispatch model (304) treats a read box as a producer:
at pool startup the runner pushes the read box's value into every
consumer slot wired from it. After that single push, the read box
is "done" — it never pushes again.

This breaks down in two cases:

1. **Multiple read boxes feeding the same input slot.** The slot
   ends up holding one value (whichever read box's startup-push
   landed first) and silently ignores the others. The user's
   intent — "rotate among these values" — is not honored.
2. **A read box feeding a slot whose other producers fire many
   times.** Once the read box's startup push is consumed, the slot
   sits empty until a call-box producer fills it. If the consumer
   fires N times and the call-box producer also fires N times,
   the read box's value is consumed at most once. The user's
   intent — "this is a default that's always available" — is not
   honored.

## Intended behavior

A read box is an **inexhaustible source**, not a one-shot producer.
Its declared value is always available, on every consumer's
request. Consumers pull from read boxes when they need a value and
no value is currently waiting in the slot.

### Pull, not push

Read boxes do not push at startup, do not appear in the pool's task
queue, and do not have task structs of their own. They are
**callable sources** whose state lives in the box's runtime record:

- For an inline literal (`value` field set), the value sits in the
  box's record as bytes ready to copy.
- For a file path (`path` field set), the bytes are read once at
  graph load and cached in the box's record. The user editing the
  file mid-run is not supported; the cached bytes are the source of
  truth for the run.

The attempt-task model from the architecture doc makes the pull
happen naturally. When an attempt task examines a consumer's input
```

75	
76	These are the same fields 312's same-language fast path
77	reserves; this issue is the one that requires every spec to
78	**actually implement them**, driven by the data-box use case.
79	(312 needs them too, but its driver is performance; 317 needs
80	them for correctness — a data box can't function without them.)
81	
82	### The rule, restated
83	
84	The author of a data box writes a `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` /
85	`.<whatever>` file that returns or produces a value using
86	*their language's native idioms*. No SoraMech macros. No
87	SoraMech includes. No SoraMech-specific JSON shape constraints
88	beyond "what your spec's native_to_json can convert."
89	
90	If the author returns a Lua table, the Lua spec's
91	`native_to_json` converts it. If they return a C struct, the C
92	spec's `native_to_json` walks it according to the type declared
93	in the box. If they print JSON-ish text from a bash script, the
94	bash spec's `native_to_json` is a no-op identity (the script
95	already produced JSON).
96	
97	The contract belongs to the spec author, not the box author.
98	That's the inversion: **SoraMech bends to the language, not the
99	other way around.**
100	
101	## Native handle representation
102	
103	The shape of `src` / `dst` in the callbacks is per-spec. Each
104	spec documents what it accepts:
105	
106	- **Lua spec**: `src` is the index of a value already pushed
107	  onto the worker's `lua_State`. `src_size` is unused. The
108	  spec's `native_to_json` reads the value at that stack index
109	  and writes JSON. `json_to_native` pushes the parsed JSON value
110	  onto the stack, and `dst_size` reports the number of stack
111	  slots used (always 1 for SoraMech's flat-value model).
112	- **C spec**: `src` is a typed pointer + the box's declared
113	  output type drives the walk. `src_size` is the type's
114	  storage footprint. Mirror image for `json_to_native`. (This
115	  ties into 307's typed-wrapper-generation work — the same
116	  type system describes both the wrapper signature and the
117	  JSON shape.)
118	- **Bash spec**: `src` is text the script produced.
119	  `native_to_json` validates and reshapes to JSON if needed (a
120	  bash script that prints `42` becomes JSON `42`; a script that
121	  prints `{"a":1}` passes through unchanged). `json_to_native`
122	  is similarly a near-identity. The spec author is encouraged
123	  to require scripts produce well-formed JSON in the first
124	  place rather than write a bash-syntax parser.
125	
126	The dispatch layer doesn't know or care which representation a
127	spec uses. It only ever passes `src` and `src_size` to the
128	spec's callback. The spec is opaque to the runtime; that's the
129	whole point.
130	
131	## Data box semantics
132	
133	A data box's `data` field becomes optional. The box gains
134	optional `lang` and `ref` fields:
135	
136	```json
137	{
138	  "id": "config",
139	  "kind": "data",
140	  "lang": "lua",
141	  "ref": "config.lua"
142	}
143	```
144	
145	At graph load, the runner:
146	
147	1. Resolves the spec for `lang`.
148	2. Calls the spec's `init` (already done at worker startup).
149	3. At box-fire time, asks the spec to execute the `ref` file
150	   and produce a native value (this is essentially the same
151	   "load this file, run it, get its return value" path the
152	   spec already implements for call boxes — call boxes call a
153	   named function; data boxes take the file's return value).
154	4. Calls the spec's `native_to_json` to convert the native
155	   value to JSON text.
156	5. Pushes that JSON text down the wire as the box's output —
157	   same as today's data path, just with the value sourced
158	   from a language-native file.
159	
160	If the box has no `lang` and a literal `data` field, the legacy
161	inline-data path applies unchanged.
162	
163	## Why every spec must implement these
164	
165	The same JSON the data box emits is consumed by some downstream
166	box (call, comparator, file_write, whatever). That downstream
167	box, if it's in a different language, will eventually need
168	*its* spec's `json_to_native` to convert the JSON back. So the
169	two callbacks are paired — every spec that participates in the
170	graph needs both. A spec that implements `native_to_json` but
171	not `json_to_native` can only be data-box sources; a spec with
172	only `json_to_native` can only be call-box sinks. We require
173	both so any language can play any role.
174	
175	Specs that haven't been updated yet should set both callbacks
176	to a stub that returns -1 with a clear error message ("spec
177	`<name>`: native_to_json not implemented; this box's value
178	can't be routed cross-language"). The hard-fail makes the gap
179	loud rather than producing wrong output.
180	
181	## Suggested implementation steps
182	
183	1. Extend the `lang_spec_t` declaration in
184	   `langs/lang-spec.h` if not already done by 312's
185	   infrastructure work. (Per the round summary, the slot is
186	   reserved.)
187	2. Implement `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` in each
188	   spec:
189	   - **Lua spec**: walk the value at the stack index. Use the
190	     existing C JSON writer (`libs/json/json.c`). Handle Lua
191	     tables both as arrays (1-indexed contiguous integer keys)
192	     and as objects. Refuse functions, userdata, threads.
193	   - **C spec**: depends on 307's typed-wrapper machinery. For
194	     now, a stub that only supports scalar types (int, double,
195	     string) is enough to get data boxes working in C.
196	   - **Bash spec**: validate-and-pass-through. The script is
197	     expected to produce JSON. `native_to_json` runs the JSON
198	     parser to validate; `json_to_native` is identity.
199	3. Extend the data box runtime path in `src/012-dispatch.c`
200	   (or wherever data boxes dispatch from) to consult `lang` /
201	   `ref` when set and route through the spec callback chain.
202	4. Add a fixture: `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` with a Lua
203	   data box, a C consumer, and a Bash consumer all reading the
204	   same value. The integration check asserts each consumer
205	   sees the right data.
206	5. Update each spec's `*.info.md` to document the native handle
207	   shape its callbacks expect.
208	
209	## Relevant files
210	
211	- `langs/lang-spec.h` — extend the contract
212	- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` —
213	  implement the callbacks
214	- `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.info.md` — document native handle
215	  semantics per spec
216	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — data-box dispatch path
217	- `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` — new fixture (see step 4)
218	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — same author-writes-natural-
219	  code principle; this issue extends it from call boxes to data
220	  boxes
221	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — reuses the same
222	  spec callbacks for a different (performance) reason
223	
224	## Open questions
225	
226	- Bash's `json_to_native` is a near-identity because the
227	  scripts already deal in text. Is there value in having it
228	  validate and reformat for consistency? Probably yes; cheap
229	  insurance against malformed downstream input.
230	- Should the Lua spec auto-detect array-vs-object tables, or
231	  require the box author to annotate? Auto-detect (contiguous
232	  1-indexed integer keys → array; otherwise object) is what
233	  every other lua-to-json library does and is intuitive enough.
234	- C spec's `native_to_json` for arbitrary structs is a real
235	  rabbit hole. Tie scope to 307 — the same type declarations
236	  drive both the wrapper and the JSON walk. Until 307 lands,
237	  only scalar types are supported for C data boxes.
238
