## Assistant

Let me actually unpack the "Lua incompatibility" carefully, because I think I framed it too narrowly the first time.

**What the bridge contract says**

`lang_bridge_fn` is `(handle, src, src_size, dst, dst_capacity, dst_size)`. Bytes in, bytes out, with a handle on the side. That contract treats bytes as the universal medium.

**What Lua actually does inside the contract**

Lua's `native_to_json` reads from the top of `lua_State` and writes JSON to dst. It ignores src/src_size.
Lua's `json_to_native` parses src as JSON and pushes the value onto `lua_State`. It ignores dst/dst_capacity.

So Lua's bridge isn't byte-to-byte — it's value-on-stack-to-bytes and bytes-to-value-on-stack. The signature is the same; the semantics aren't.

**But this isn't actually a Lua-specific problem.**

The deeper thing is: Lua values don't have a canonical byte form. A Lua table has no fixed bytes — it's a hash table living in `lua_State`, and the only universal serialisation we picked was JSON. A Python `dict` would have the same problem. A Ruby hash, a JavaScript object, anything from a runtime that owns the value's lifetime — same. The byte-buffer assumption was always a property of "stateless byte-handlers" (C, Bash), not a property of "languages in general."

So if we want true agnosticism, the question isn't "how do we accommodate Lua?" — it's "does the system's contract assume buffer-orientation, and should it?"

**The fact that re-frames everything**

Look at what each spec's `invoke` actually does today on a JSON-tagged input:

- **Lua**: `parse_json_to_stack` → fall back to `lua_pushlstring` if parse fails. Already auto-detects.
- **C**: `try_strip_json_primitive` → fall back to raw bytes if parse fails. Already auto-detects.
- **Bash**: ignores the flag entirely; bytes are bytes for bash.

Every spec already does the "try JSON, fall back to native" dance internally. The `input_native[i]` flag is only an *optimization hint* that lets Lua skip the JSON parse attempt when dispatch knows the cell came from a same-language producer. It saves maybe 100ns per call on a same-lang Lua→Lua wire. It doesn't change semantics anywhere.

The `output_native` flag is similar — it tells Lua to use `tostring` when all consumers are same-lang. But that "fast path" is *broken* for tables (`tostring(table)` returns `"table: 0x..."`), so any honest Lua function that returns a table has to JSON-encode anyway. The flag is buying performance that mostly isn't usable.

**What dropping the flags really costs and buys**

Drop both flags and have every spec auto-detect:

- Cost: same-lang Lua→Lua pays a ~100ns JSON-parse-attempt-then-fallback per input. Lua→Lua with table returns starts working correctly (was broken). The "fast path" goes away.
- Buy: spec contract goes from `invoke(..., input_data, input_sizes, input_native, n_inputs, output_native, ...)` to `invoke(..., input_data, input_sizes, n_inputs, ...)`. Every future spec — Python, Ruby, anything — drops in without special-casing or runtime-class branching. The dual-ring slot's per-cell ring tag stops being load-bearing at the spec level (it's still useful for ordering).

**Where it really gets agnostic**

Once the flags are gone, the dual-ring slot itself becomes vestigial *for format purposes*. Its surviving job is ordering — the `RING_JSON` / `RING_NATIVE` tag was both "this is the format" and "this is the position in the producer sequence". The format part stops mattering; the position part still matters for parallel-iterator ordering.

We could either:
- **Keep dual-ring as-is**, with the per-cell tag becoming advisory/unused at the spec interface. Code stays, complexity stays in the slot store, but the spec contract is clean.
- **Collapse dual-ring back to single-ring tagged**, undoing slice 1. Bigger teardown, cleaner result.

**The honest summary**

Lua's "incompatibility" with the buffer contract is real but it's not the load-bearing thing. The load-bearing thing is: **the per-input/per-output flags are an optimisation that mostly doesn't work in practice and force every spec to branch on something it could auto-detect.** Removing them is the agnosticism move you're asking about.

The amendment was right in spirit; what it called "dispatch normalises every input to native bytes" was really pointing at "every spec auto-detects per input." That doesn't require dispatch to do any conversion at all — it requires the spec contract to stop pretending it knows the per-cell format.

So the three paths re-stated:

1. **Narrow** (drop just the dead slots): preserves the per-cell flags as load-bearing. Doesn't actually fix the agnosticism question.
2. **Full agnostic** (drop the flags, specs auto-detect, dual-ring becomes advisory or gets collapsed): real spec contract simplification, fixes the broken Lua-table same-lang case, opens the door to drop-in future runtimes.
3. **Collapse dual-ring all the way to single-ring tagged**: most aggressive teardown, also addresses the vestigial dual-ring complexity.

Now that I see it that way, my lean has shifted toward (2) — auto-detect is what every spec is already doing, the flags are an optimisation that doesn't earn its complexity, and the "Lua incompatibility" disappears not because we accommodate Lua specially but because no spec needs the format hint.

## Assistant

The worker-affinity thing you spotted is the foundation, and I want to make sure I name it correctly before going further:

**Workers don't share runtime state.** Each Lua worker has its own `lua_State`. The task that produced a value ran on worker A; the task that consumes it might land on worker D. Worker D's `lua_State` is a fresh hash table — worker A's stack is invisible to it. So *even for same-language wires, the value has to leave its runtime and re-enter on the consumer side.* The wire is between workers, not between language states.

That means "leave the value on the stack" is never an option. The only universal medium between workers is bytes. The "fast path" question is just **what bytes** — JSON, raw text, msgpack, whatever the spec picks — not whether to serialise. Serialisation always happens.

Given that, the per-edge native/JSON distinction is just about **format choice per outgoing wire**. The producer knows (at graph-load time) whether each consumer is same-language. If same, write the spec's preferred native byte form. If different, write JSON. The consumer's auto-detect handles both cases without a per-cell tag:

- Cell carries `hello` (raw Lua-native string): consumer tries JSON parse → fails (no quotes) → falls back to lua_pushlstring. Consumer gets the string.
- Cell carries `"hello"` (JSON from C producer): consumer tries JSON parse → succeeds → gets string.
- Cell carries `42`: parses as JSON number either way. Both spec forms are bit-identical here.
- Cell carries a table: producer can only have written JSON (Lua's "native form for tables" *is* JSON; tables have no other byte form). Consumer parses → gets table.

The per-cell format tag in the dual-ring slot was only ever needed if we wanted to **skip the JSON parse attempt** in the consumer when we knew the producer wrote native. That's the 100ns optimisation I mentioned. It doesn't change semantics anywhere.

On your "I don't think either of these will work" — can you say more about which part feels wrong? Because the third option you might be gesturing at — **drop the dual-ring slot entirely, single-ring data + TAGGED variant for iterator ordering, producer picks format per edge at push time, consumer auto-detects per pop** — is what falls out if we accept the worker-affinity argument. The native/JSON ring split exists in code today but it isn't load-bearing once the consumer is doing auto-detect anyway.

A couple of places I'm not sure about and want to surface:

- **What does "Lua native bytes" mean for primitives?** A Lua number written as `42` is also valid JSON. A Lua string `hello` written raw isn't valid JSON. So the "native" form for primitives is a strict subset / variation of JSON. We could just say: Lua's spec writes JSON-form bytes always (numbers as `42`, strings as `"hello"`, tables as JSON), and the "native" optimisation is purely conceptual — the bytes are JSON-shaped either way. That removes the spec's job of deciding "native vs JSON" at all.
- **What about closures, userdata, threads?** These have no byte representation. The sentinel primitives in issue 318 (`$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque`) are the answer — they encode "this is a Lua thing only Lua can reconstruct" as a JSON-shaped sentinel. Cross-language wires that carry one of these fail loudly at the consumer's bridge. We don't need to design that here; it's another issue's problem.

So I think we're close to alignment: bytes are the wire; producer picks the cleanest form it can per outgoing edge; consumer auto-detects. What's the part you think won't work?

## User

How does lua interact with shared memory? The type of shared memory we're using for this project.

## Assistant

The honest answer for the slot store this project uses:

**Lua doesn't interact with the shared memory at all, except as bytes.**

Each pool worker has its own `lua_State`. LuaJIT's hard rule is that a `lua_State` is owned by exactly one OS thread — you cannot touch it from another thread concurrently. The Lua GC heap (where tables, strings, closures live) is private to that one `lua_State`. So `lua_State` A on worker 0 and `lua_State` D on worker 3 have no shared Lua-level memory. They are, from each other's perspective, completely separate Lua universes that happen to be running the same source files.

What they CAN share is the project's byte-buffer layer:

- The slot store's cells (`slot_push` / `slot_pop` move bytes through ring buffers with per-slot spinlocks; the unified allocator refcounts the cells so they stay valid while any worker holds a reference).
- The large-value heap (the same refcount machinery, for variable-size payloads).

Both are managed at the C level. Workers read and write bytes; the spinlocks and atomics handle concurrency. Lua sees these only when its spec hands it a `(void *, int)` pair pointing into a cell, and Lua does one of two things with it:

- `lua_pushlstring(L, ptr, size)` — Lua copies the bytes into its own GC-managed string. After that, the slot cell can be released; Lua's string is independent.
- `parse_json_to_stack(L, ptr, size)` — Lua walks the bytes, constructs an equivalent Lua value in its own GC heap, pushes it. Same deal: after the call, the slot cell is decoupled from anything Lua holds.

In both cases, **the byte buffer's lifetime ends at the Lua call**. Lua never holds a pointer into a shared slot cell; it copies what it needs into its own state.

**LuaJIT FFI** is the one place this could be different. With FFI, Lua code can read shared memory directly via `ffi.cast(...)` without copying. We could expose slot bytes to Lua and let user code read them as a `cdata` pointer. But:

- The user's Lua function would have to call FFI explicitly. The wire-level contract still hands the spec bytes, not Lua values.
- FFI'd bytes are still bytes from Lua's perspective. You can't `pairs()` over an FFI pointer and get table semantics — Lua values are what live in `lua_State`'s GC heap, not in the C buffer.
- The slot cell's stable-address window (between alloc and unref) would have to extend across the FFI call's life, which is a real refcount discipline question.

So FFI is an optimisation for *avoiding the copy*, not for *sharing Lua values*. Even with FFI in the picture, a Lua table that goes from worker A to worker D has to serialise into bytes and deserialise into a new table in D's heap.

**This isn't Lua-specific.** Python's `dict`, Ruby's `Hash`, JavaScript's `Object` — every runtime that owns its values has the same constraint. The runtime's heap is private; the only way values cross threads is bytes. The agnosticism win you're chasing is: the system's contract assumes bytes, every spec knows how to turn its values into bytes and back, and no spec ever needs to know anything about another spec's runtime.

**Implication for the dual-ring slot:**

The slot store as it stands is byte storage. The dual-ring split added a per-cell format tag (native vs JSON), but format is information about how to *interpret* bytes — and JSON is self-describing while raw text falls through auto-detect. The format tag isn't a property of the memory; it's a property of the bytes themselves. Putting it in the slot's metadata duplicates what the bytes can carry. That's the architectural smell I think you've been pointing at.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The agnosticism question dissolves once you accept that shared memory is bytes-only for any runtime that owns its values. The slot store is exactly right at the layer it sits — it's the unified byte medium between workers, with refcounts handling lifetime and spinlocks handling concurrency. What slice 1 of 312 added on top (per-cell format tag, two data rings) was an attempt to encode interpretation hints in the memory layout, but interpretation belongs in the bytes (JSON is self-describing) or in the consumer's auto-detect (raw bytes flow through). The slot store doesn't need to know about formats; the specs do, and they already do.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

So: what bytes need to live in shared memory? Whatever bytes the producer's spec wrote. What's the consumer's job? Turn those bytes back into a value in its own runtime. Everything else — the dual rings, the format tag, the per-input flag in the typedef — is the system encoding information that belongs elsewhere.

99	## Current behavior
100	
101	Today every wire's bytes are whatever the producing spec wrote, and
102	the consuming spec reads them verbatim. The Lua spec emits JSON via
103	`lua_native_to_json` (`langs/lua/spec.c:335`); the C and Bash specs
104	have no bridge implementations yet and only carry whatever bytes
105	their bytes-in-bytes-out `invoke` produced.
106	
107	The dispatch layer picks the spec callback (`invoke_native` /
108	`invoke_json` / fallback `invoke`) based on a single per-box
109	`use_native_invoke` bit computed at graph load — true only when
110	**every** input edge and **every** output edge of the box stays
111	within one language. That's the pessimistic per-box approximation:
112	a box whose output fans to one same-language consumer and one
113	cross-language consumer is classified as cross-language for the
114	whole box, so the same-language neighbor eats the JSON cost too.
115	
116	The wire-format design question — what shape the slot has when
117	same-language hops should stay native and JSON should only show up
118	at language borders — has not been settled in code. This issue
119	settles it.
120	
121	## Intended behavior — dual-ring slot
122	
123	**Each input slot is two ring buffers plus an ordering ring.**
124	
125	```
126	slot {
127	    ring_native  : ring of cells holding consumer-lang's native bytes
128	    ring_json    : ring of cells holding JSON bytes (cross-language values)
129	    ring_order   : ring of (which_ring, idx) tuples in arrival order
130	    ... existing peek / pop modes apply to ring_order ...
131	}
132	```
133	
134	**Producer push** (per outgoing connection):
135	
136	- If the connection's per-edge bit `output_edge_native[j]` is true,
137	  the producer's spec writes its native bytes to the consumer
138	  slot's `ring_native`.
139	- Otherwise the spec converts to JSON via `native_to_json` and
140	  writes to `ring_json`.
141	- Either way, push a `(which_ring, idx)` tuple to `ring_order`.
142	
143	**Consumer pop**:
144	
145	- Pop `ring_order` to learn which ring the next value lives in.
146	- Pop the cell at the indicated index in the indicated ring.
147	- The consumer's spec receives a `(native | json, bytes)` pair per
148	  input and picks the matching decoder.
149	
150	This shape is precise on **mixed fan-in ports**: a port fed by one
151	Lua producer and one C producer keeps the Lua values native in
152	`ring_native` and the C-translated-to-JSON values in `ring_json`.
153	The consumer pays JSON-decode cost only on cross-language cells,
154	not on the entire port. The per-port-classification alternative
155	(slot is all-JSON if any fan-in is cross-lang) is the simpler
156	fallback — strictly correct, but forces native producers to eat
157	JSON on mixed-fan-in ports. Dual-ring keeps each cell at the
158	right cost.
159	
160	### Per-language native bytes
161	
162	Each spec picks its own native byte format. The only contract is
163	internal symmetry: the spec's own native-write and native-read have
164	to round-trip its own runtime values.
165	
166	- **Lua** — bytes the spec can deserialize back to a Lua value
167	  quickly. Candidates: msgpack, `lua_dump` for closures, a custom
168	  binary form. `cjson.encode` is a valid starting point (a JSON
169	  string the Lua spec parses with `cjson.decode`); refine later if
170	  profiling shows the encode/decode is hot.
171	- **C** — `memcpy` of the typed struct. The C spec's generated
172	  wrapper (issue 307) knows the layout, so the ring cell holds the
173	  struct's bytes verbatim.
174	- **Bash** — strings. Bash has no other native form; its native
175	  ring stores string bytes directly.
176	
177	### Why JSON for the cross-language ring
178	
179	JSON's structure is self-describing — six kinds (object, array,
180	number, string, bool, null), each with an obvious mapping into
181	every language's runtime. Adding a new language K means K's spec
182	implements one pair of bridges: `native_to_json` and
183	`json_to_native`. No spec ever needs code that knows another
184	language's types. The N-language matrix collapses to N spec-pair
185	implementations, not N² pair-wise paths.
186	
187	The harder cases — closures, opaque language values, function
188	pointers — don't fit JSON's structure. JSON carries them via
189	sentinel primitives:
190	
191	- `$ref` — pointer + length + type tag for bytes in the large-value
192	  heap. In-process specs deref directly; cross-process boundaries
193	  inline the bytes at the UDS marshaller.
194	- `$function_pointer` — in-process address + signature for
195	  callables. Wire compile fails if the consumer's language can't
196	  reconstruct.
197	- `$lang_opaque` — "these bytes are language-X-internal." The
198	  destination spec either knows what to do or wire compile fails.
199	
200	First-cut behavior: `encode_value` in the Lua spec hard-errors on
201	function / userdata / thread (`langs/lua/spec.c:317`). Sentinels
202	land in a later slice.
203	
204	## Per-edge classification at graph load
205	
206	The graph loader's single-threaded phase walks every connection
207	and computes per-box arrays:
208	
209	- `input_edge_native[i]` — one bit per input port. True iff every
210	  producer feeding that port shares the consumer's language.
211	- `output_edge_native[j]` — one bit per outgoing connection. True
212	  iff the consumer on the other end shares this box's language.
213	
214	Both arrays are already declared and populated — see
215	`src/010-graph-loader.h:145` and `src/010-graph-loader.c:689-754`.
216	
217	Under dual-ring, only `output_edge_native[j]` is consulted on the
218	push path (it picks the ring at write time). The consumer-side bit
219	`input_edge_native[i]` is now informational — the actual per-cell
220	ring tag lives in `ring_order` and is precise per cell.
221	
222	The per-box `use_native_invoke` summary is **removed**. The
223	dispatch reads `ring_order` per pop and consults the cell's tag
224	directly.
225	
226	### Why per-edge, not per-box
227	
228	The earlier per-box approximation collapsed on the first
229	cross-language edge it found — a Lua box with one C neighbor went
230	JSON across all its other edges. Per-edge keeps the cost local to
231	the actual cross-language boundary. The interior of any
232	same-language island skips JSON; only the boundary edges pay. The
233	implementation cost is identical: same number of bits per box,
234	stored per-port / per-connection instead of per-box.
235	
236	Dual-ring takes precision one step further: even on a single port
237	with mixed fan-in, each individual cell is in the right ring. The
238	boundary tightens from "edges that cross languages" to "cells
239	that crossed languages."
240	
241	## Runtime dispatch
242	
243	### Input-read
244	
245	For each input port:
246	```c
247	(which_ring, idx) = ring_order_pop(slot->ring_order)
248	cell_bytes        = ring_at(which_ring == NATIVE ? slot->ring_native
249	                                                  : slot->ring_json,
250	                            idx)
251	input_data[i]   = cell_bytes
252	input_native[i] = (which_ring == NATIVE)
253	```
254	
255	`input_native[i]` is a per-input flag the spec consults to know
256	whether `input_data[i]` is its native form or JSON.
257	
258	### Spec invoke
259	
260	**Superseded by the Amendment (2026-05-22)** — the final design
261	drops the per-input and per-output flags from invoke and moves
262	format conversion into dispatch. The historical signature
263	below is what slices 2–4 implemented; the next slice removes
264	the flags.
265	
266	The spec's `invoke` signature gains per-input and per-output flags:
267	
268	```c
269	int invoke(handle, file_path, fn_name,
270	           const void **input_data, const int *input_sizes,
271	           const int *input_native, int n_inputs,
272	           int output_native,
273	           void *out_buf, int out_buf_capacity, int *out_size);
274	```
275	
276	The spec dispatches per input: if `input_native[i]`, decode as
277	native; else decode as JSON. The output is written in whatever
278	form `output_native` indicates — one form, one write. The previously
279	separate `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` callbacks collapse into
280	this single dispatching `invoke`; the per-input dispatch is what
281	the design actually needs.
282	
283	For boxes whose output fans to multiple consumers in different
284	languages, the dispatch picks one form for `output_native` based
285	on whichever ring is dominant (typically native if any
286	same-language consumer exists; the cross-language consumers eat a
287	`native_to_json` conversion at push time). The detail is a runtime
288	optimization, not a correctness concern.
289	
290	### Output-push
291	
292	```c
293	for each outgoing connection j:
294	    if output_edge_native[j]:
295	        push native_bytes      to consumer->ring_native
296	        push (NATIVE, idx)     to consumer->ring_order
297	    else:
298	        if output_was_native: json_bytes = spec->native_to_json(native_bytes)
```

## Assistant

Three threads to pull on. Let me handle the sentinel point first, then check the pre-amendment plan, then come back to the dual-ring question.

**On sentinels**: you're right, and the failure mode is worse than I framed it. A sentinel like `$function_pointer:0x7f...` only makes sense to the runtime where that address lives. If lua_State A creates a closure and the sentinel rides the wire to a consumer task that lands on worker 3 with lua_State D, the address is meaningless — and even if we used some abstract reference (a registry key), the closure object exists only in A's GC heap. D's heap has no such closure.

The only way sentinels work for closures is if the closure is **registered in every worker's lua_State by name** — e.g., a named function in a module that every worker loaded at init. Then `$function_pointer:my_module.greet` is a portable reference because every worker has its own copy of `my_module.greet`. That's not really a sentinel for a runtime value; it's a name lookup, and it only works for things that were declarable up front. Anonymous closures, runtime-created tables with closures inside, etc., genuinely can't cross. They have to fail loudly at the bridge.

So sentinels narrow down to "named, pre-loaded-on-every-worker things." Useful for module functions; useless for runtime-created closures. Issue 318 should be honest about that constraint.

## Assistant

Found it. Let me walk through the pre-amendment plan because I think it actually answers your worry directly.

**Pre-amendment plan (the original 312 design, ~mid-May)**

Slot has three rings:
- `ring_native` — bytes in the consumer's spec's own native format
- `ring_json` — JSON bytes (only used at cross-language boundaries)
- `ring_order` — `(which_ring, idx)` tuples in arrival order

Producer push, per outgoing connection:
- If `output_edge_native[j]` is true (consumer is same-language), the spec writes its native bytes to `ring_native`.
- Else, the spec converts via `native_to_json` and writes to `ring_json`.
- Either way, push `(which_ring, idx)` to `ring_order`.

Consumer pop:
- Pop `ring_order` to learn which ring the next cell lives in.
- Read the cell from that ring.
- Spec gets a `(format, bytes)` pair per input and uses the matching decoder.

**Critically: each spec defines its own native byte format.** The plan was:
- **Lua**: bytes the Lua spec can deserialise back to a Lua value quickly. Started with `cjson.encode` for simplicity, with msgpack or a custom binary form listed as the optimisation target if profiling showed the encode/decode was hot.
- **C**: `memcpy` of the typed struct (wrapper from 307 knows the layout).
- **Bash**: strings (its only native form).

Cross-language ring is JSON because JSON is self-describing and every spec implements one pair of bridges. N specs = 2N bridge implementations, not N² pair-wise paths.

**Why this answers your "do we always have to JSON?" worry**

No. JSON is only the **cross-language** wire format. Same-language wires use the spec's own native format, which is whatever bytes that spec can read back fastest. For Lua → Lua, that doesn't have to be JSON — the current `cjson.encode` was a placeholder, not the final answer. msgpack or a custom binary Lua-value codec would be faster.

So the architecture you're defending — "different format in different location, deterministic, fewer branches" — *is* the original plan. The amendment came later and tried to collapse it (move format conversion into dispatch, drop the per-input flag, have specs auto-detect). We established earlier why that doesn't fit Lua's stack-based bridge. **The amendment was the wrong turn.** Walking it back puts us on solid ground.

**On sentinels and closures**

You're right and I want to be more precise. The pre-amendment plan listed three sentinel kinds:

- `$ref` — pointer + length into the large-value heap. This works because the LVH is shared memory; any worker can deref the pointer. Fine.
- `$function_pointer` — in-process address. Falls apart for Lua because the address is into one `lua_State`'s GC heap. Only works for things registered by name in every worker's state (module functions). For runtime-created closures, no.
- `$lang_opaque` — "these bytes are language-X-internal." Only the same-spec consumer can read them. Useful for, say, Lua's own native byte form passing through a wire labelled `$lang_opaque` because it's a black box to anyone else. Not really a closure transport mechanism.

So sentinels work for `$ref` (shared memory pointers) and `$lang_opaque` (same-spec only). They don't solve closures-across-states. That problem is unsolvable in general because runtime values don't share GC heaps. The honest answer in 318 has to be: closures cross only via named registration, runtime-created closures can't be wired cross-state, the bridge fails loudly.

**Proposed 312 closeout**

Given all this, the right delivery is:

1. **Revert the amendment in the issue body.** Re-affirm the pre-amendment dual-ring design. The current code already implements it (slices 1–4.5); the amendment was a paper change that the code never followed.
2. **Drop the dead `invoke_native` / `invoke_json` slots from `lang_spec_t`.** Slice 5 collapsed dispatch's branching to a single `invoke`; the extra slots are vestigial.
3. **Add the dispatch-level mixed-fan-in test** the original plan called for — one cell arriving JSON-tagged and one arriving native-tagged on the same port, consumer reads them in arrival order, each cell decoded with the right path.
4. **Document the open optimisation**: Lua's "native bytes" is currently `cjson.encode` (JSON-shaped). Replacing with msgpack or a binary Lua-value codec is a future enhancement that would make same-lang Lua-Lua wires faster than they are today. Out of scope for closing 312; tracked as a follow-on.

This commits to the architecture you've been pointing at, removes the architectural dead-end the amendment created, and the only structural code change is dropping two unused struct slots. Want me to proceed?

## Assistant

Let me give you the honest picture. It's medium-sized, well-contained, and the value is structural rather than behavioral.

**What the refactor actually does**

Today's dispatch action is two-phase:

```
producer task runs:
  read inputs from slots
  invoke spec
  for each outgoing connection:
     push output to consumer slot
     dispatch_spawn_if_ready(consumer)   ← may malloc a new task struct
                                            and submit it to the pool
  free this task
```

The attempt-task model collapses to one shape:

```
attempt task runs:
  if every required input is ready (slot has value, or read predecessor):
     snapshot inputs
     invoke spec
     for each downstream consumer this attempt just wrote to:
        slab_alloc → new attempt task for that consumer
        submit to pool
  return; slab_free this task either way
```

The two shapes both work. The differences:

- One task struct shape instead of two roles for the same struct.
- One allocation path (slab pop from a free list) instead of `malloc` per spawn.
- The "is this consumer ready?" check moves from the producer's epilogue to the consumer attempt's prologue. Same check, different timing.
- An attempt that finds inputs not ready returns without doing work; the next push to any of that consumer's slots triggers a fresh attempt.

**Code scale**

Touches one file mostly: `src/012-dispatch.c`. Probably 300-500 lines of churn — restructuring the action body, swapping malloc for the existing 302 slab allocator, deleting the inline spawn-if-ready calls. Two struct definitions change (task struct grows; gets a free-list link). One header. A handful of test references.

The task struct change is the most visible part:

```c
// today
typedef struct { int box_id; int task_id; void *ctx; } dispatch_task_t;

// after
typedef struct {
    void   *next_free;
    box_t  *box;
    uint32_t task_id;
    uint32_t flags;
    void   *output_destinations;
    void   *input_snapshot;
    uint32_t live_wire_count;
    uint32_t _pad;
} attempt_task_t;
```

The 64-byte layout is designed to fit one cache line and to use the slab allocator from 302 — which already exists and is in use elsewhere.

**What it buys**

- One allocation path instead of `malloc` per spawn. Real perf win on hot graphs (thousands of tasks per second). Today every spawn does a small `malloc`, every free does a small `free`.
- The "task" abstraction stops carrying two meanings (a check that might run, a worker that will run). Reading the dispatch becomes shorter.
- The retirement hook (`live_wire_count` — counts how many outgoing wires of a box are still in use, drives box-level cleanup) gets its placeholder in the cell layout, ready for a future slice.

**What it does NOT buy**

- Zero observable behavior change. Every fixture passes today and would pass after.
- No new routing kinds, no new features for users.
- No performance win unless the graph is allocation-hot.

**Risk profile**

- Medium. Touches the central dispatch loop, the single most thread-sensitive piece of the system. Any bug here is potentially a race or a leak.
- Mitigated by: existing test coverage is decent (10 dispatch tests, 11 integration fixtures including the multi-language pipeline). A regression should surface quickly.
- The slab allocator is already battle-tested (used by slot cells, large-value heap, dispatch buffers). Using it for task structs is a known-good path.

**My honest framing**

It's the kind of refactor that's worth doing if:
- The current `malloc`-per-spawn is showing up in a profile.
- You're about to touch the dispatch action for some other reason (new routing kind, attempt-time bookkeeping, etc.) and want the substrate clean first.
- You want the structural retirement hook in place because a future feature (box-lifetime tracking, run statistics) needs it.

Worth deferring if:
- The current code works and you're focused on user-visible features.
- There's no profile data saying allocations are hot.
- The phase-3 demo is more important than internal cleanliness.

I'd lean **defer** for now. 304 in code already does everything the user can see; the refactor is paying down architectural debt that isn't currently charging interest. The strategems we've been writing argue against churning code without a concrete trigger. When the trigger arrives — a profile, a feature, a race — that's the right moment to land the attempt-task shape.

What's your read? The trigger could be anything from "I want to time my pipeline runs and see allocations dominating" to "I want the retirement hook for run statistics".

## Assistant

You're asking exactly the right questions, and they reframe the refactor.

**On malloc and shared memory**

`malloc` returns memory from the process heap. The process heap is shared across all threads in the process — that's a property of Linux's process model, not of malloc specifically. glibc's malloc is thread-safe via internal locking (or per-thread arenas with cross-arena access via locks for cross-thread reads). So a `malloc`'d pointer can be passed from worker A to the pool queue, picked up by worker D, dereferenced safely. That's what `dispatch_spawn` is doing today and it works.

So the current malloc IS in shared memory. The slab allocator from 302 is *also* in shared memory — it's a global pool with its own locks, just amortised across spawn/free pairs to skip the per-call malloc overhead. Both shapes work; slab is faster on hot graphs because it skips the malloc bookkeeping.

The "we need everything accessible from any thread" check is satisfied either way. Stack memory wouldn't be (it dies with the thread frame). Thread-local storage wouldn't be (per-thread). But process-heap memory — whether from malloc or from a slab allocator that itself sits in the process heap — is fine.

**On the signal question — this is the load-bearing one**

You're seeing something I glossed over. Let me re-examine.

In the **current (epilogue) model**:
- Producer's action runs. Reads inputs, invokes spec, pushes outputs to consumer slots.
- For each consumer it touched, the producer calls `dispatch_spawn_if_ready(consumer)`:
  - Checks if the consumer has all its required inputs (via `box_is_ready`).
  - If yes, `malloc`s a task struct, submits to pool queue.
  - If no, returns silently. Some *other* producer's later push will trigger another check.
- The pool worker picks the task up and runs it.

This is the model in the code today. The signal mechanism is exactly what you described: producer writes inputs, asks "is the consumer ready?", submits iff yes.

In the **proposed attempt-task model**:
- Producer's action runs. Same as above through the push.
- For each consumer touched, the producer submits an attempt-task *without checking readiness*.
- The attempt-task runs. *First thing it does* is check readiness. If not ready, returns without invoking. If ready, snapshots inputs, invokes, etc.

You're right that the attempt model wastes work on the not-ready case — every producer push to a 3-input consumer with one wire still pending submits an attempt that wakes up, looks around, sees inputs not ready, and quits. The epilogue check skips that whole round-trip.

**So the user's question is correct: why move the check?**

Reading the original amendment again with your framing, the conceptual benefit was "one task shape, one allocation, no separate readiness step." But the current model *already has one task shape* (`dispatch_task_t`), *one allocation path* (`malloc` in `dispatch_spawn`), and the readiness check is just a few lines of C in the producer's loop — not a separate task kind.

The amendment was solving a problem that didn't really exist. The "check is a separate task" framing was an artifact of how the design got written down, not a property of the code.

**Re-scoped: what's actually worth doing**

If we strip the attempt-task rewrite of its conceptual ambition and ask what's left:

1. **Use the slab allocator instead of `malloc` per spawn.** Real perf win on hot graphs; trivial code change (`malloc(sizeof *t)` → `ua_alloc(...)`, paired `free` → `ua_unref`). ~10 lines total. No semantics change.

2. **Maybe expand the task struct to add a `live_wire_count` field.** Placeholder for future box-retirement work. Optional, only if you want it sitting there for the next slice. ~5 lines.

3. **The "one task shape, no inline check" rewrite.** Doesn't actually buy what the original framing claimed. Defer indefinitely.

(1) is so small it doesn't need to be its own issue — it's a perf-tweak on an existing path. (2) is design-level; only do it if there's a concrete next slice that needs the field. (3) is the thing I was overselling; skip it.

So the answer to "is it a big change or small": **the actually-useful piece is small.** The big piece I described turns out to be solving a non-problem once you look at what the current code already does.

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The conversation we just had is a small instance of the strategem I wrote earlier — "describe the runtime, not the paperwork." The issue body framed the current model as "two-phase, check separate from work" and the refactor as "unified attempt-task model." But the runtime doesn't actually have two phases or a separate check task; it has one task with a few lines of readiness logic inline. The issue's framing exaggerated the structural distance between current and target to justify a refactor that, on inspection, was mostly cosmetic.

Your question — "what causes the prologue to initiate?" — cut straight to the runtime. Once we trace the actual signal flow, the answer is "the producer's epilogue submits, same as today," which means the refactor's main feature (move the check) wasn't moving anything load-bearing. The smell I should have caught earlier: any time a refactor description says "X moves from A to B" and X is a few lines of code rather than a structural unit, ask whether the "from A to B" is real or paper. Here it was paper.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Want me to update 304's body to be honest about this, and either close it or scope-tag the remaining items (slab swap + maybe `live_wire_count` placeholder) as the actual deliverables?

## Assistant

312 closed earlier this session — moved to `issues/completed/`. Here's where it landed:

**Status (closed)**

- Dual-ring slot foundation: shipped (slice 1, 2026-05-21). Three rings per slot — `ring_native`, `ring_json`, and an ordering ring that sequences cells across mixed fan-in. `slot_push_native`, `slot_push_json`, `slot_pop_ordered` API.
- Per-edge classification: shipped. The graph loader computes `input_edge_native[]` per input port and `output_edge_native[]` per outgoing connection. Producer picks ring at push time; consumer reads ring at pop time from the ordering tag.
- Per-cell ordering tag: shipped. Iterator-fed wires propagate the iterator counter as a tag so parallel iterator workers' pushes land in invocation order at the consumer.
- Spec contract: single `invoke` entry point with per-input / per-output flags, plus `native_to_json` / `json_to_native` bridges. The dead `invoke_native` / `invoke_json` function-pointer slots in `lang_spec_t` are gone.

**What the closeout commit did this session**

The big move was **walking back the 2026-05-22 amendment**. That amendment had proposed dispatch normalising every input to native bytes before invoke, dropping the per-input flag, having every spec see one buffer shape. We figured out it assumed buffer-oriented bridges — works for C and Bash, doesn't work for Lua (whose `json_to_native` pushes onto `lua_State` rather than writing to a buffer) or for any future stateful runtime. The amendment forced stateful runtimes into a contract that didn't fit. We reverted it.

The architectural lesson — "a contract must fit every implementer, not just the simplest" — got its own strategem: `strategems/contracts-must-fit-every-implementer.md`.

**Code change in the closeout commit**

Small. Two dead struct slots removed from `lang_spec_t` (`invoke_native`, `invoke_json` had been unused since slice 5 collapsed dispatch's branching). One new dispatch test (`dual_ring_per_cell_format`) that proves the per-cell ring tag flows from producer push through `read_inputs` to the consumer spec's `input_native[i]` flag — push the same bytes via `slot_push_native` and `slot_push_json` in two halves, observe a Lua consumer's `M.describe` returning `"string:..."` vs `"table:42"`.

**One future enhancement, noted out-of-scope**

Lua's "native bytes" today is JSON-shaped (via the same encoder as `native_to_json`). The pre-amendment design said the spec could pick any native byte format it can read back fast — msgpack, `lua_dump`, custom binary. Replacing today's JSON-shaped output with one of those would make same-language Lua-to-Lua wires cheaper. Contract supports the swap; no fixture exercises it today; left for a profile-driven slice later.

**Suite at closeout**

17 test groups green including the new dual-ring dispatch test, all bridge tests, the integration pipeline that exercises Lua → C → Bash with real JSON crossing language boundaries.

Anything specific about 312 you wanted to look at — the slot store internals, the per-edge classification rules, the bridge contract, the strategem? Or were you scoping for the next move?

1	# 313 — Research: whole-program same-language compilation
2	
3	## Status
4	in progress — Lua slice landed 2026-05-21 (compile-time
5	concatenation in `soramech-compile.sh`, runtime detection in the
6	Lua spec, end-to-end test). C and Bash merges are follow-ons.
7	
8	## Concept
9	
10	Instead of one box = one function call going through the dispatch
11	layer + slot store + spec invocation, **merge all same-language
12	boxes at compile time into a single translation unit** for that
13	language. Box-to-box transitions within a language region become
14	ordinary function calls — the optimizer can inline across them, the
15	language runtime stays warm, and the slot store and dispatch layer
16	don't run for those transitions at all. Only cross-language wires
17	retain the slot machinery.
18	
19	For C: every C box becomes a function in one big `.c` file, with
20	the build emitting one `.so`. Wire endpoints become direct calls;
21	the optimizer inlines based on its usual heuristics.
22	
23	For Lua: every Lua function gets pre-compiled into one bytecode
24	chunk loaded into one shared `lua_State` per worker. Wire
25	endpoints become direct `lua_pcall`s into that chunk — no JSON
26	encode/decode, no slot push/pop, no spec re-entry per call.
27	
28	For Bash: probably no benefit — Bash is already process-per-call;
29	fusing scripts doesn't help much. Skip.
30	
31	## Relationship to 312 — what stays, what thins
32	
33	312 defines the **wire format**: dual-ring slots (native ring,
34	JSON ring, ordering ring) per input port, per-edge classification
35	at graph load, JSON-only-at-borders, native-otherwise. Every
36	value movement between boxes goes through this machinery.
37	
38	This issue's merge thins the **spec invocation overhead** — the
39	dlsym + bridge + lua_pcall (or equivalent) that happens once
40	per box invocation. For boxes in the merged region, the dispatch
41	holds a direct pointer to the function inside the merged module
42	and calls it without going through the spec interface.
43	
44	The slot machinery stays alive in the merged region. Why:
45	**boxes still run as separate thread-pool tasks**. A producer
46	on worker 0 and a consumer on worker 1 still need slots to hand
47	off the value across threads. The thread pool's parallelism
48	depends on tasks being scheduled independently; collapsing
49	same-language hops to synchronous function calls would defeat
50	the pool entirely. The merge wins on the per-call indirection
51	cost, not on the wire format.
52	
53	So a box invocation inside the merged region looks like:
54	
55	1. Worker picks up the task.
56	2. Dispatch reads inputs from the box's slots (same as 312 —
57	   dual-ring native side).
58	3. Dispatch jumps to the merged function directly (this is
59	   what 313 wins).
60	4. Function runs, returns a value.
61	5. Dispatch writes output to consumer slots (same as 312).
62	
63	Steps 2 and 5 are unchanged from 312. Step 3 is the savings.
64	
65	## Why this is research, not a feature
66	
67	Three open questions that need code-level investigation before
68	the design is concrete:
69	
70	1. **Loop and iterator boundaries**: a same-language region with an
71	   iterator inside it has dynamic re-spawning. Is the iterator
72	   merged in (a loop in the generated code) or does it remain a
73	   spec-mediated boundary? If the latter, the merge buys less.
74	2. **Compile time**: whole-program optimization is slow. Does the
75	   incremental-compile model (issue 309's mtime check) still apply,
76	   or do edits require a full rebuild of the merged region?
77	3. **Debug story**: a stack trace inside the merged region needs to
78	   point back to a specific box. Either source-map-style mapping or
79	   per-function preserved names — both have costs.
80	
81	The right way to answer these is to prototype: take a small all-Lua
82	graph (like the hello map) and see what a merged version looks
83	like, both for runtime cost and for build-system complexity. The
84	prototype should be able to fall back to the per-box path if the
85	merge fails for any reason.
86	
87	## Why blocked until rest of phase 3 completes
88	
89	The merge optimization piggybacks on the per-box model — same
90	specs, same compile callback, same dispatch layer for cross-language
91	wires. Building it before the per-box model is settled risks
92	designing against a target that's still moving. After phase 3 ships
93	and the per-box path is stable, the merge becomes a clean
94	"everything except spec invocation gets shorter for this region."
95	
96	It's also genuinely lower priority than getting the runtime working
97	at all. The same-language fast path (issue 312) captures the
98	~2x JSON-skip win without any of this complexity. The merge wins a
99	further factor only if profile data shows function-call overhead
100	matters at all after 312 ships.
101	
102	## Scope of the research output
103	
104	The deliverable is a written design (not code) with:
105	- Concrete answers to the three questions above.
106	- A worked example: the merged C generated from a 5-box C-only
107	  graph, showing what the build artifact looks like.
108	- Estimated runtime cost of the per-box path vs the merged path
109	  on that example.
110	- A recommendation: ship as a feature, ship as an opt-in flag, or
111	  shelve indefinitely.
112	
113	After the design is reviewed, a separate implementation issue
114	opens (or doesn't, depending on the recommendation).
115	
116	## Relevant files
117	
118	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — the immediate JSON-
119	  skip win this builds on
120	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — `compile` callback that would
121	  grow whole-program-aware semantics
122	- `issues/306-lua-language-spec.md` — Lua-side bytecode pre-compile
123	- `issues/309-build-system.md` — how the merge integrates with the
124	  compile/package step
125	
126	## Implementation log
127	
128	### Lua slice — 2026-05-21
129	
130	`scripts/soramech-compile.sh` grows a `merge_lua_sources` step
131	between source-copy and per-spec packaging. For every `.lua` file
132	in the map's `src/`, the merger wraps the file's body in a closure
133	and binds the closure's return value to `merged[basename]` of a
134	single output table. The result lands at `compiled/src/__merged__.lua`
135	alongside the original files (which stay untouched, so any caller
136	that hasn't been told about the merge still works).
137	
138	`langs/lua/spec.c` grows `load_via_merged()`, called on the
139	cache-miss path of `lua_invoke`. The helper derives a candidate
140	`__merged__.lua` path next to the requested source file; if the
141	file exists, it loads the merged module once per worker (cached
142	under `LUA_MERGED_CACHE_KEY` in the Lua registry), then indexes
143	the returned table by the source file's basename to recover the
144	per-file module. Lookup misses (no merged file, or no matching
145	basename inside it) fall back to per-file lazy load — the
146	existing path stays as the safety net. The dispatch never knows
147	about the merge; the spec redirects transparently.
148	
149	A unit test in `tests/306-lua-spec-test.c` exercises the merged
150	path on a synthetic temp directory: it writes a `__merged__.lua`
151	that exports a sub-module under basename `calc` and invokes a
152	phantom `calc.lua` file (no such file on disk). The invoke
153	succeeds, proving the merged path is reached. The existing
154	"compile pipeline (portable run)" integration test verifies the
155	end-to-end flow: `soramech-compile.sh` produces the merged file
156	when packaging the `pipeline` fixture, the compiled artifact
157	runs from `/tmp`, and the output matches what the source-tree
158	runner produces — the merge is transparent.
159	
160	Deferred to follow-on slices:
161	
162	- **C merge.** Concatenating C source needs care with static
163	  vs. non-static functions, file-scoped declarations, and header
164	  inclusion. Plausible approach: wrap each box's source in an
165	  anonymous namespace-equivalent (C doesn't have namespaces, but
166	  static-by-default at file scope + unique mangled exposure of
167	  the box's entry function would do it). Real work; not done.
168	- **Bash merge.** Bash already runs one persistent subprocess
169	  per worker (issue 308's UDS-server model); the "merge" would
170	  source every box's `.sh` file into the same subprocess
171	  environment at server startup. Smaller surface than C; sized
172	  somewhere in between.
173	- **Inline-into-dispatch optimization.** This slice loads the
174	  merged module per worker but still goes through the spec's
175	  `lua_pcall` for every box invocation. The big optimization the
176	  issue body talks about — turning box-to-box transitions into
177	  direct function calls inside the merged module — needs
178	  dispatch-layer work that hasn't started. The current slice is
179	  the foundation: load-once-per-worker. The direct-call
180	  optimization rides on top.
181	
182	## Open questions (for the research, not for now)
183	
184	- Cross-language wires inside the merged region: the merged region
185	  still has cross-language outputs that exit to other regions. The
186	  spec interface still applies at those boundaries, so the merge is
187	  region-internal optimization, not a global rearchitecture.
188	- C ABI for inlined wrappers: with whole-program optimization the
189	  wrapper-per-box generation might collapse to nothing. If the
190	  optimizer can prove a wire endpoint is always called from the
191	  merged region with known types, it can skip the wrapper.
192	- Iterators-with-side-effects: an iterator self-rescheduling its
193	  own task is normally dispatch-layer machinery. Inside a merged
194	  region, it could become a `while` loop. Whether that's safe
195	  depends on whether other tasks are blocked on the iterator's
196	  output slots — which they shouldn't be in a same-language region,
197	  but the analysis has to confirm.
198	
```

## Assistant

313's status, honestly read:

**Stated scope** — it's labelled `research / blocked` in phase progress and says "the deliverable is a written design (not code) with: concrete answers to three open questions, a worked example, runtime-cost estimate, and a recommendation: ship / flag / shelve."

**What actually shipped** — May 21, the Lua slice went well beyond research:

- `scripts/soramech-compile.sh` grew a `merge_lua_sources` step that wraps every map `.lua` file into closures inside one `compiled/src/__merged__.lua`.
- `langs/lua/spec.c` got `load_via_merged()` on the cache-miss path — derives the merged file's location, loads it once per worker, indexes by basename. Misses fall back to per-file lazy load (safety net).
- `tests/306-lua-spec-test.c::test_invoke_via_merged_module` proves the merged path is reached.
- The compile-pipeline integration test verifies the merged file is produced when packaging the `pipeline` fixture and the compiled artifact still runs.

**What didn't ship**

- **C merge** — concatenating C source files with name-mangling, static-scope handling, and header dedup. Real work.
- **Bash merge** — but the persistent-subprocess server from 308 effectively already does this. Every worker's bash process loads all scripts at server startup. The "merge" is functionally redundant for Bash.
- **The inline-into-dispatch optimisation** — the *big* win the body describes: turning box-to-box transitions into direct function calls inside the merged region, bypassing spec invocation per call. Hasn't started.

**Reading what the body claims the merge buys**

Step 3 of the issue's "what a merged box invocation looks like" loop is "Dispatch jumps to the merged function directly (this is what 313 wins)." Steps 2 and 5 — input read / output push — are unchanged from 312. So the merge's gain is just the spec-invocation indirection (dlsym + bridge + lua_pcall through the spec interface). That's a few hundred nanoseconds per call at most. The body acknowledges this directly: *"boxes still run as separate thread-pool tasks. ... The merge wins on the per-call indirection cost, not on the wire format."*

That's a smaller win than "merge everything" first reads as.

**My honest read**

The Lua slice that shipped is the practical part — keeping the lua_State warm with all box modules loaded, skipping the per-file `luaL_loadfile + pcall` on cache miss. That's real, real-world useful, and it's done.

The three open research questions:
1. *Iterator boundaries* — only matters if we do inline-into-dispatch. Without that, iterators stay dispatch-layer routing, no change.
2. *Compile-time cost* — for Lua, none (concatenation only, no optimisation). For C if we ever do it, real cost. For Bash, nothing.
3. *Debug story* — Lua's slice keeps per-file basenames in the merged module's table keys, so traces still attribute correctly. Solved for Lua.

The genuinely open question is whether inline-into-dispatch is worth pursuing. Reading the body's own analysis — pool parallelism depends on tasks being scheduled independently across workers, and collapsing wire transitions to synchronous function calls would defeat the pool — the answer points at *no*. The merge can't actually skip the slot machinery without breaking parallelism, so the win is bounded to the spec-invocation indirection, which is small.

**Three honest paths**

1. **Close 313 now**: write the research recommendation directly into the issue body — "Lua module merging shipped; C merge deferred until profiling shows need; Bash merge is functionally done via 308's persistent server; inline-into-dispatch shelved because it conflicts with the parallelism model" — and move to completed.

2. **Trim 313 to just the Lua slice**: split the C / inline-into-dispatch piece off as a separate future issue. Close 313 with what shipped.

3. **Keep 313 open** as ongoing research, no action needed.

My lean is **(1) — close it with the recommendation written in.** Phase 3 is essentially done, the Lua practical part shipped, and the bigger merge optimisation has a real architectural conflict (parallelism vs synchronous calls) that the body itself surfaces. Writing that conflict down as the recommendation is honest research output.

What's your read?

---
# 248 — Encapsulated map as box

## Status
open

## Concept

A map directory is normally opened via the map-select UI as the
top-level graph being edited. **This issue adds a second way to
open a map:** through the file browser, as an *encapsulated box*
on the currently-edited parent map. The opened map becomes a
single box on the parent's canvas. Its inputs come from input
ports inside the sub-map that have been marked as
externally-supplied; its outputs come from the sub-map's
`write` boxes, packed into a single byte-blob with a leading
size index.

Encapsulation is a function-like abstraction over a sub-graph.
The parent doesn't see the sub-map's internals; it sees one box
with declared input ports, declared output ports, and known
semantics. The sub-map can be edited independently, reused across
parent maps, and packaged once for distribution.

## Marked input ports inside the sub-map

A **data** box inside the sub-map (kind: `data`, the existing
language-agnostic file-IO source) can be marked as
**externally-supplied** via a small toggle on the box's
inspector. Toggling declares: "this data box's value comes from
the encapsulating context, not from its `path` or `value`
field."

The toggle is intentionally scoped to `data` boxes — those are
the natural entry points for external values into a map. Call
boxes / read boxes / write boxes don't carry the toggle, which
keeps the inspector uncluttered for the common cases. Each
external input becomes a small shared-memory chunk seeded by
the encapsulating call's invoke; the marked data box reads from
that chunk on each fire, treating it identically to a value
loaded from disk.
```

212	Two implementation paths exist. The encapsulating box being a
213	normal `call` box from the editor's perspective doesn't pick
214	between them; the choice lives in what the map-language spec
215	does behind the scenes and what (if anything) the compile
216	pipeline does at build time.
217	
218	### Path A — Compile-time inlining
219	
220	The compile pipeline (issue 309) recognizes encapsulating call
221	boxes and **expands them into their sub-graphs at build time**.
222	Steps the compile step takes per encapsulating box:
223	
224	1. Load the sub-map's graph from `ref`.
225	2. Rename every internal box with a parent-namespaced id:
226	   `stats_pipeline__compute`, `stats_pipeline__filter`, etc.
227	3. Copy every internal wire across, with the renamed ids.
228	4. For each marked data box inside the sub-map: replace it with
229	   a wire that brings the encapsulating box's matching input
230	   value directly to wherever the data box's output was wired.
231	5. For each write box inside the sub-map: route its emitted
232	   value into the packed-blob writer that produces the
233	   encapsulating box's output (or, alternatively, expose each
234	   write box's value as a separate output wire that downstream
235	   consumers can wire to individually — see "Output unpacking
236	   under inlining" below).
237	6. Remove the encapsulating call box from the parent's graph.
238	
239	After expansion, the compiled artifact is **one flat graph**.
240	The runtime never sees `lang: "map"`. The map-language spec
241	exists only for the editor's sake (validating refs, listing
242	the sub-map's inputs/outputs for the inspector).
243	
244	**Pros:**
245	
246	- No new runtime machinery. The dispatch, slot store, and spec
247	  registry all operate on flat graphs as today.
248	- Best per-call performance — no sub-graph startup cost, no
249	  nested dispatch.
250	- **Composes cleanly with issue 313** (whole-program same-
251	  language merge). The inlined sub-graph's same-language regions
252	  merge into the parent's merge as if they were always one map.
253	- Debugging stack traces inside the inlined region point to
254	  flat box ids — readable once you know the naming convention.
255	
256	**Cons:**
257	
258	- The sub-map gets baked into every parent that uses it. Edit
259	  the sub-map → every parent must recompile.
260	- No hot-reload of sub-maps. The editor would have to
261	  re-expand on every parent map edit, or accept that the
262	  parent's last-compiled artifact lags the sub-map source.
263	- Compile time grows with sub-map size × number of
264	  encapsulations.
265	- Deep recursion (sub-maps inside sub-maps inside sub-maps)
266	  inflates the parent's compiled output proportionally.
267	- Output unpacking under inlining is awkward: the packed-blob
268	  output format makes sense at a boundary; with no boundary,
269	  downstream consumers either wire to individual sub-write-box
270	  outputs (more efficient but a different mental model) or get
271	  the packer's blob baked in too (the packer is just another
272	  inlined function call).
273	
274	### Path B — Runtime sub-graph dispatch
275	
276	The encapsulating box stays as one box in the parent's flat
277	graph. The map-language spec's `invoke` runs the sub-graph at
278	call time:
279	
280	1. **`map_init(worker_idx)`** — load every sub-map referenced
281	   by the running program (or lazily on first invoke). Each
282	   worker gets its own copy of the sub-graph state (slot store,
283	   dispatch context).
284	2. **`map_invoke(handle, sub_map_dir, ..., inputs, ..., out_buf, ...)`** —
285	   for each input, write the bytes into the shared-memory chunk
286	   that the matching marked data box reads from. Then drive the
287	   sub-graph to quiescence using the existing pool's dispatch
288	   loop (the sub-graph's tasks are normal pool tasks distinguished
289	   by a nested namespace). When quiescent, walk the write boxes,
290	   read their emitted values, pack them into the size-index +
291	   data-section blob, write the blob to `out_buf`.
292	
293	The sub-graph's parallelism uses the same thread pool as the
294	parent's. A sub-graph's tasks compete for workers alongside the
295	parent's tasks. From the pool's perspective, the difference is
296	just box-id prefix.
297	
298	**Pros:**
299	
300	- Sub-map loaded once per worker, reused across many parent
301	  invocations and across many parent maps.
302	- Hot-reload friendly: change the sub-map, parent picks up the
303	  new version on next run (no parent recompile).
304	- The packed-blob output format is the natural shape — the
305	  boundary exists, the spec packs at the boundary, downstream
306	  consumers unpack with the helper library.
307	- Debugging stack traces preserve the sub-map identity (the
308	  task's namespace says "this came from `stats-pipeline`").
309	- Recursion is natural — the spec's invoke can nest-invoke its
310	  own spec for a sub-sub-map.
311	
312	**Cons:**
313	
314	- New runtime machinery: the map spec's `invoke` runs a nested
315	  dispatch loop. Either the dispatch layer grows a re-entrancy
316	  contract, or the map spec replicates enough of the dispatch
317	  machinery to drive the sub-graph itself.
318	- Each encapsulating-box call pays sub-graph startup cost —
319	  seeding inputs, possibly resetting slot state from a previous
320	  invocation, collecting outputs at the end. Small but real.
321	- Does NOT compose with issue 313's same-language merge — the
322	  sub-map's source can't merge with the parent's, because at
323	  build time they're separate compilation units. (313 could be
324	  extended to merge across encapsulation boundaries, but that's
325	  a separate design.)
326	- Re-using the sub-graph across invocations requires reset
327	  semantics. Either the sub-graph's slots and counters get
328	  reset between invocations (cost), or each invocation gets a
329	  fresh sub-graph instance (allocation cost).
330	
331	### What to weigh
332	
333	The choice turns on whether encapsulation is **a build-time
334	abstraction (like a C macro or a Lua-function-inlined-by-LuaJIT)**
335	or **a runtime composition (like calling a function in a shared
336	library)**.
337	
338	Path A treats it as a build-time abstraction. Encapsulation is
339	how the user organizes source; the compiled artifact is one
340	flat program. Best for raw performance and integration with
341	the existing machinery. Cost: rebuild propagation.
342	
343	Path B treats it as a runtime composition. Encapsulation is a
344	real boundary at execution time, with the packed-blob output
345	as the wire format across the boundary. Best for modularity,
346	hot-reload, and clear semantics. Cost: per-call overhead and
347	loss of cross-boundary optimization.
348	
349	A third option exists — **support both, picked per encapsulating
350	box** via a small flag in the box JSON (`encap_mode: "inline" |
351	"runtime"`). The compile pipeline expands inline-mode boxes;
352	runtime-mode boxes stay encapsulated. Each user picks per
353	encapsulation based on their needs. Higher implementation cost
354	but maximally flexible. Probably not in the first slice; flag it
355	for later if either A or B alone proves too narrow.
356	
357	Recursion (a sub-map encapsulates another sub-map) works under
358	either path. Cycles between maps are a compile-time (or
359	load-time, under B) error.
360	
361	## Relationship to existing issues
362	
363	- **Issue 207 (source-file-browser)** — the file browser is the
364	  entry point for the "encapsulate" action.
```

## Assistant

248's relationship to 313 is direct and is actually called out in 248's body. Here's the short version:

**248 in one line**: a sub-map becomes a single box on a parent map's canvas, exposing marked-as-external `read` boxes as inputs and `write` boxes as packed outputs. Sub-graphs become reusable, composable units.

**Two implementation paths in 248, and they differ exactly on 313**

**Path A — Compile-time inlining.** The compile pipeline expands the encapsulating box into its sub-graph at build time: rename internal boxes with a parent namespace, copy every internal wire, replace marked external-reads with direct wires from the parent's matching inputs, replace write boxes with the packed-output writer. Encapsulating box vanishes. Result: one flat graph at runtime. The runtime never sees `lang: "map"`.

This **composes cleanly with 313** — and the body says so directly: *"The inlined sub-graph's same-language regions merge into the parent's merge as if they were always one map."* Because once the sub-graph is inlined into the parent, its Lua boxes and the parent's Lua boxes look indistinguishable to 313's merger; they go into the same `__merged__.lua`. The encapsulation boundary disappears at compile time, so the language-merge boundary also disappears.

**Path B — Runtime sub-graph dispatch.** The encapsulating box stays one box. The map-language spec's `invoke` runs the sub-graph at call time, via a nested dispatch loop. Sub-map loaded once per worker; sub-graph's tasks compete for workers alongside the parent's tasks; outputs collected and packed at quiescence.

This **does NOT compose with 313** — body again: *"The sub-map's source can't merge with the parent's, because at build time they're separate compilation units."* The sub-map keeps its own compiled artifact (its own `__merged__.lua`); 313's merge stops at the encapsulation boundary.

**The relationship summarised**

- Path A treats encapsulation as a build-time abstraction. 313's same-language merger then operates across the (now flat) parent + inlined sub-graphs, getting more value because there's more same-language code to merge.
- Path B treats encapsulation as a runtime composition. 313's merger stops at every encapsulation boundary; each sub-map has its own merge.

The body even floats a Path C: a per-box `encap_mode: "inline" | "runtime"` flag so each encapsulation picks. That gives 313 the benefit on inline-mode boxes and isolates runtime-mode ones.

**One other thing worth flagging while we're here**

248 still uses `kind: "data"` in its body (the old terminology). Issue 229 renamed `data` → `read`/`write` long ago, and 244 made read boxes pull-on-demand. The body's "marked data boxes" should be "marked read boxes," and the description of how external inputs flow into them — *"a small shared-memory chunk seeded by the encapsulating call's invoke; the marked data box reads from that chunk on each fire"* — is essentially 244's read-box `cached_value`, just seeded by the parent at invoke time instead of by graph load. The terminology drift would be worth fixing if/when 248 moves forward.

**Where you are with 248**

It's open. No implementation. The path decision (A vs B vs both) is the load-bearing question; everything else (UI, file layout, output packing) waits on that. Scoping it would mean either picking a path or building Path C's flag-based "support both."

What angle are you on it from — picking the path, scoping the work, or just mapping how the issues connect?

## User

Can you tell me, how would it look if we did both? For example, if the user can pick a map at runtime to load and run, versus if they know at compile time a couple that they want to include as well. Can we do both systems, and accept both possibilities?

## Assistant

Yes, this works cleanly — and your framing of the two use cases is exactly the lens for picking per-encapsulation. Let me sketch what "both" actually looks like.

**The flag and the two use cases**

The encapsulating box's JSON carries an `encap_mode` field:

```json
{
  "id": "stats",
  "kind": "call",
  "lang": "map",
  "ref": "maps/stats-pipeline",
  "encap_mode": "inline",          // or "runtime"
  "inputs":  [...],
  "outputs": [...]
}
```

- `"inline"` → "I know this sub-map at compile time, I want it folded into the parent, and I want 313 to merge across the boundary."
- `"runtime"` → "I'm picking this sub-map by name at run time, or I want it hot-reloadable, or I want it isolated."

A user's typical run-time case maps to this: a `read` box feeds a name string into the encapsulating box; the spec resolves "load `maps/<that-name>`" lazily. The parent can't know the name at compile time, so `inline` isn't an option — `runtime` is forced.

A user's typical compile-time case: the user dragged a known sub-map from the file browser onto the canvas. The reference is static; `inline` is the natural default; the compiled artifact stays one piece.

**What the compile pipeline does for each**

`scripts/soramech-compile.sh` grows a graph-rewrite pass *before* the existing source-merge and per-spec packaging. For every encapsulating box in the parent:

- If `encap_mode == "inline"`:
  1. Load the sub-map's graph.
  2. Namespace every internal box id (`stats__filter`, `stats__compute`).
  3. Copy every wire across with the renamed ids.
  4. For each marked external-input `read` box in the sub-map, replace it with a direct wire from the parent's matching input on the encapsulating box.
  5. For each `write` box in the sub-map, either pack into a blob (preserving the boundary's output shape) or expose individually as separate outputs from the encapsulating-box site.
  6. Delete the encapsulating box from the parent's box list.
  7. Recurse: if the sub-map itself contains inline encapsulations, expand those too.

After this pass, the parent's flat graph is what the rest of the pipeline (and 313's merge step) sees. 313's `merge_lua_sources` then groups same-language boxes — including the ones that came from the inlined sub-map — into one module.

- If `encap_mode == "runtime"`:
  1. Leave the encapsulating box alone.
  2. Also compile the sub-map's own artifacts (its own `__merged__.lua`, its own C `.so`s) into a side directory the parent's runtime can find.
  3. The encapsulating box stays a `lang: "map"` call box in the parent's flat graph.

A single parent can have both. The pipeline iterates every box, decides per-box, runs the right transform.

**What the runtime does for each**

For `inline` boxes: nothing special. They're already flat in the compiled artifact; the dispatch sees ordinary boxes.

For `runtime` boxes: the map-language spec — `langs/map/spec.so` — implements the standard `lang_spec_t`:

- `map_init(worker_idx)`: empty cache of loaded sub-graphs.
- `map_invoke(handle, box, sub_map_path, fn_name, inputs, ..., out_buf, ...)`:
  1. Resolve the sub-map (cache key = path or runtime-supplied name). On miss, `graph_load(path)`, `graph_attach_runtime(...)` against a *fresh* per-worker slot store dedicated to this sub-graph instance.
  2. For each input, seed the matching marked external-`read` box's `cached_value` with the bytes the parent supplied.
  3. Drive the sub-graph to quiescence on the *same pool* — its tasks share workers with the parent. Per-task box ids get a nested-namespace prefix so traces stay distinguishable.
  4. Walk the `write` boxes, collect their captured outputs, pack into the size-indexed blob format, write to `out_buf`.
  5. Optionally reset slot state for re-invocation (cheaper than re-loading the whole sub-graph).

This is exactly Path B from 248's body, modular and self-contained.

**Where 313 sits in the picture**

- Inline-mode boxes contribute their source files to the parent's `__merged__.lua`. 313's existing merger handles them uniformly — it doesn't even need to know about encapsulation; it just sees more boxes after the inline pass ran.
- Runtime-mode boxes get their own `__merged__.lua` in their own compiled directory. 313 merges within each sub-map's region but doesn't cross the boundary.

**The schema constraint, which "both" doesn't escape**

Even in runtime mode, the parent needs to wire the encapsulating box's input ports and output channels at compile time. So the encapsulating box's JSON must declare the schema: input port names, output channel names, output blob layout. Sub-maps loaded at runtime have to *match* that schema (the spec checks at load time; mismatch is a fatal error).

This is like a function-pointer typedef in C — you can swap which function the pointer holds at run time, but the signature is fixed in source. For maps, the analogue is: the encapsulating box says "this sub-map will have inputs A/B/C and outputs X/Y," and any sub-map referenced by name at runtime has to satisfy that contract.

**Cost summary, honest**

- The map-language spec is built either way (it's the foundation for runtime mode and the dynamic-load case is your stated motivating use).
- Inline-mode is a graph-rewrite step in the compile pipeline. Probably 100–200 lines of clean code: load the sub-map, namespace, splice, recurse. Lives in `soramech-compile.sh` or a sibling.
- The encap_mode flag is one field in the box JSON, one branch in the rewrite pass, one branch in the dispatch's "is this a real call or a runtime sub-graph?" decision (and the latter is just "what spec answers this `lang`").
- Output packing/unpacking is the same helper library either way (used at the boundary in runtime mode, used at the inlined `write`-replacement in inline mode if the user wants the blob format).

So "both" is roughly **Path B's work plus a graph-rewrite pass for Path A's transform**. It's bigger than either path alone, but the *runtime* surface area is just Path B's spec; everything Path A adds happens at compile time and disappears before the runtime sees the graph. That separation keeps "both" from compounding complexity at the hot path — the dispatch loop never branches on `encap_mode`.

**One last thing worth surfacing**

If `encap_mode` is a per-box choice, the user can mix freely in one parent. Stats-pipeline as inline (perf-critical, known at compile time); user-template-loader as runtime (dynamic, hot-reloadable). The semantics are the same from the parent's perspective — both look like a call box with declared inputs and outputs. The difference is invisible to the parent's other boxes; it only shows up in build output (one is gone, one stays) and in the runtime profile (one calls inline, one calls through the map spec).

That clean separation — "both modes look the same to the rest of the parent" — is what makes "both" cheaper than it sounds. The encapsulating box's *contract* is the same; only the implementation behind it varies.

What's your read — is the schema-at-compile-time constraint OK for the runtime use case you have in mind?
